Reasoning about Recursive Quantum Programs DOI: 00.0000/0000000.0000000 Journal: TOCL Volume: 00 0 000 0 CCS: Computer systems organization Embedded systems CCS: Computer systems organization Redundancy CCS: Computer systems organization Robotics CCS: Networks Network reliability
Zhaowei Xu, Mingsheng Ying, Benoît Valiron

Abstract
. Most modern (classical) programming languages support recursion. Recursion has also been successfully applied to the design of several quantum algorithms and introduced in a couple of quantum programming languages. So, it can be expected that recursion will become one of the fundamental paradigms of quantum programming. Several program logics have been developed for verification of quantum 𝐰𝐡𝐢𝐥𝐞 \mathbf{while} -programs. However, there are as yet no general methods for reasoning about (mutual) recursive procedures and ancilla quantum data structure in quantum computing (with measurement). We fill the gap in this paper by proposing a parameterized quantum assertion logic and, based on which, designing a quantum Hoare logic for verifying parameterized recursive quantum programs with ancilla data and probabilistic control. The quantum Hoare logic can be used to prove partial, total, and even probabilistic correctness (by reducing to total correctness) of those quantum programs. In particular, two counterexamples for illustrating incompleteness of non-parameterized assertions in verifying recursive procedures, and, one counterexample for showing the failure of reasoning with exact probabilities based on partial correctness, are constructed. The effectiveness of our logic is shown by three main examples — recursive quantum Markov chain (with probabilistic control), fixed-point Grover’s search, and recursive quantum Fourier sampling.



A new assertion logic for verifying quantum programs with probabilistic control

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

Quantum computation nowadays has become a hot topic in computer science.
One of the fundamental incentives of this research direction is
to have successfully designed several quantum algorithms, particularly,
Shor’s algorithm
(
Shor 1994
)
and Grover’s search algorithm
(
Grover 1996
)
,
which by employing the intriguing and unnatural effects of quantum mechanics,
e.g. superposition and entanglement,
can obtain significant computational advantages.
The design of quantum algorithms is mainly based on the slogan “quantum data and classical control",
that is, that the data could be superposed and even entangled,
which can be manipulated by basic quantum operations — unitary evolution and measurement,
but the high-level control is still classical
(either deterministic or probabilistic, e.g. case, loops, etc).
(For recent discussions about quantum control,
i.e. superposition of quantum programs or superposition of quantum processes,
see, e.g.,
(
Badescu and
Panangaden 2015
)
,
(
Chiribella 2012
;
Chiribella et al. 2013
)
,
and Chaps. 6 and 7 of
(
Ying 2016
)
.)

Quantum programming with recursion.

Classical recursion, as a high-level control structure,
has been applied to quantum algorithm design,
and brought substantial advantages to quantum computation.
Instead of speaking about quantum algorithms with while-loop control
(as examples of tail recursion, say,
Shor’s algorithm
(
Shor 1994
)
and Grover’s search algorithm
(
Grover 1996
)
),
we shall exemplify quantum algorithms with general recursion.
The first representative is Grover’s fixed-point search algorithm
(
Grover 2005
;
Yoder
et al. 2014
)
,
which, by applying (mutual) recursion,
provides his famous/popular original search algorithm
(
Grover 1996
)
with an advantage
— converging monotonically to the target state.
As another typical example,
recursive quantum Fourier sampling
(
Bernstein and
Vazirani 1997
)
requires exponentially fewer queries than the classical one,
and has extensive applications in research on quantum complexity theory
(cf. the Introduction of
(
McKague 2012
)
).
This quantum algorithm is described by a recursive procedure with pointer passing and ancilla qubits.
As a third example, recursive quantum Markov chain is
a quantum extension of Etessami and Yannakakis’s Recursive Markov chains
(
Etessami and
Yannakakis 2009
)
,
and can be used to simulate a multi-player game with probabilistic control
(
Feng
et al. 2013b
)

(to be the running example of this paper).

Implementation of quantum algorithms, i.e. quantum programming,
has been extensively investigated for the past two decades (also following the slogan “quantum data and classical control"),
including both high-level (imperative) and low-level (functional) programming languages
and their semantics
(
Altenkirch and
Grattage 2005
;
Ömer 2003
;
Sabry 2003
;
Sanders and
Zuliani 2000
;
Selinger 2004a
)
,
as surveyed in
(
Selinger 2004a
;
Gay 2006
;
Ying 2016
)
.
In particular,
recursive procedures with pointer passing have already been introduced by Selinger
in his high-level quantum programming language
\mathrm{QPL}

(
Selinger 2004a
)
.
The literature
(
Ying 2016
)
defined
a quantum
\mathbf{while}
-language with recursion and local variables (to describe ancilla quantum data).
In the last few years,
a number of mature low-level quantum programming languages have been developed,
e.g., Quipper
(
Green et al. 2013
)
, Scaffold
(
Abhari
et al. 2012
)
,
LIQUi
|\rangle

(
Wecker and Svore 2014
)
, Q
\#

(
Svore et al. 2018
)
, and QWIRE
(
Paykin
et al. 2017
)
.

Verification of quantum programs.

In view of counter-intuitiveness of quantum effects,
quantum programming is an inherently error-prone process.
To ensure correctness and safety of quantum systems,
formal verification and formal program analysis (static approaches) is a right choice

(
Baltag and Smets 2011
;
Brunet and
Jorrand 2004
;
Ying
et al. 2017
;
Li and Ying 2018
;
Liu et al. 2019
)
,
relative to running-time (dynamic) approaches like testing and debugging,
due to a series of frustrating facts:
quantum states fail to be directly observed before measurement;
the current state could be potentially destroyed after measurement;
and the measurement result is randomly distributed.
Among those formal methods on quantum programming,
we prefer program-logic-based approaches, e.g. quantum program logic
(
Baltag and Smets 2011
;
Brunet and
Jorrand 2004
)
,
compared with model-based methods, e.g. quantum model checking
(
Gay
et al. 2008
;
Feng
et al. 2013a
;
Feng
et al. 2015
;
Ying
et al. 2014
)
,
since the former is developed in a syntax-oriented style
making it easily extensible to various program features, e.g. recursion, non-determinism, parallelism, etc.

Hoare logic has been the fundamental method for formal program verification
(
Apt
et al. 2009
)
.
The basic idea of this logic is based on the intermediate assertion method
(
Floyd 1967
;
Hoare 1969
)
,
which was originated with Alan Turing
(
Apt and Olderog 2019
)
(called Turing-Floyd-Hoare Principle).
Hoare’s approach makes (interactive) theorem proving for verifying high-level algorithmic description language
proceed at the same abstraction level as the language itself.
Thus verification using Hoare logic is more human-friendly than low-level (machine-friendly) verification.
Several Hoare-like logics for reasoning about quantum programs have been developed
(
Chadha
et al. 2006
;
Feng
et al. 2007
;
Kakutani 2009
;
Ying 2011
;
Unruh 2019b
;
Unruh 2019a
;
Barthe
et al. 2020
)
.
Among them, D’Hondt and Panangaden
(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
)

proposed a notion of quantum weakest precondition for a general quantum operation.
The attractiveness of this approach is that quantum predicates,
used as preconditions and postconditions,
are modelled by Hermitian operators and thus have a natural interpretation as physical observables.
Based on this, a quantum Hoare-like logic
for reasoning about quantum
\mathbf{while}
-language was designed
and its (relative) completeness was established, by Ying in
(
Ying 2011
)
.
In these quantum Hoare logics, we find that

(1)

Quantum predicate can merely describe a fixed property on quantum states,
and the mechanism for guaranteeing termination is not designed in a syntactical style.

(2)

These programming languages don’t support general recursion,
and there is no compositional inference rule for the structure of ancilla quantum variables.

(3)

These logics are mainly for verifying deterministic properties of quantum programs,
and at most can do reasoning with approximate probabilities.
In other words, there is no axiomatic basis for reasoning about quantum programs with exact probabilities.

1.2.
Contributions of the Paper

The aim of this paper is to propose an assertion logic for
verifying parameterized recursive quantum programs with ancilla qubits or quints
(the abbreviation of quantum int).
By using formulas of this assertion logic as pre- and post-conditions,
a quantum Hoare logic is designed for proving partial correctness, total correctness,
and even probabilistic correctness (including probabilistic termination) of those quantum programs.
The work extends D’Hondt and Panangaden’s quantum predicates and quantum weakest preconditions
(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
)
,
and Ying’s quantum Hoare logic
(
Ying 2011
)
.
Concretely speaking, five main contributions are highlighted:

•

We extends the syntax of quantum
\mathbf{while}
-language defined in
(
Ying 2011
)
with general recursive procedures.
For the formal semantics of the extended quantum programming language,
we define
a nondeterministic operational semantics
by introducing the concept of labeled transition relation,
define
a quantum-operation-directed denotational semantics
(independent of program states),
and
relate them to each other
(cf. Thm.
3.7
).
Note that the denotation of a recursive procedure can be defined as
the least fixed point of a function over quantum operations,
making the representation of this denotation have a closed form.
The formal semantics can be used to describe behaviors of quantum programs with probabilistic control
(cf. Exams.
3.3
and
3.6
).

•

By
constructing two counterexamples

(cf. Exm.
5.1
for partial correctness, and Exam.
5.2
for total correctness),
we illustrate
the failure of Hoare’s approach (i.e. intermediate assertion method) with quantum predicates as pre- and post-conditions in verifying recursive procedures
.
To extend the applicability of Hoare’s approach from while loops to general recursion
(cf. Rem.
5.7
),
we have to
introduce a parameterized quantum assertion logic

extending quantum predicates by incorporating parameters.
In the setting of this assertion logic,
we redefine the notion of Löwner order and upper (resp. lower) limit of quantum predicates,
quantum program correctness and expressiveness of intermediate assertions
developed in
(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
;
Ying 2011
)
(cf. Thms.
4.14
and
4.15
).

•

We introduce
inference rules for proving both partial and total correctness of recursive procedures

with formulas of the new assertion logic as pre- and post-conditions.
These rules usually should be used in combination with the
Substitution Rule,
dealing with substitution for parameters in pre- and post-conditions of a Hoare’s triple

(cf. Exms.
5.1
and
5.2
for counterexamples).
The rules of proving total correctness can be adapted to
proving probabilistic correctness,
i.e. reasoning with both approximate and exact probabilities
(cf. Thms.
5.4
and
5.6
),
based on the result of (general or compact) soundness and completeness
.
However,
the rules of proving partial correctness can’t be used universally for reasoning with exact probabilities,
even if the issue of nontermination is involved (cf. Rem.
5.6
for a counterexample)
.
Note that the rule for proving total correctness is purely syntactic,
and, as a special case, we obtain a syntax-directed inference rule for proving total correctness of while loops.
We also discuss the issues of synthesizing intermediate assertions and necessity of parameters
in verifying recursive procedures and while loops.
These discussions reveal that recursion is generally more complex than while loops in the setting of program logics.

•

Verification of some more sophisticated recursive quantum programs
like the example of recursive quantum Fourier sampling cannot be done by merely using the above techniques;
they further need the facilities of variable localization and parameter passing.
So, our fourth main contribution is to develop

inference rules for proving both partial and total correctness of variable localization and recursive quantum procedures with pointer passing.

To this end,
we propose
two different but equivalent inference rules for proving the two correctness of variable localization
,
and the
Adaptation Rule for dealing with the substitution of program variables in pointer passing
.
Note that previous proof rules for recursive procedures should be used jointly with Adaptation Rule in verifying parameterized recursive procedures.
The proof rule for variable localization endows the logic
with the ability of verifying a (general) quantum operation in a compositional way.
Various aforementioned results,
like Thms.
3.7
,
4.15
,
5.4
,
5.6

(and soundness and completeness results), can be extended to covering these facilities.

•

The fifth and last main contribution is to present various examples and related work.
Among these examples, we adopt recursive quantum Markov chain as the running example of the paper,
since it can fully show the ability of our logic in dealing with quantum programs with probabilistic control.
Specifically speaking, this example illustrates operational semantics, denotational semantics,
and reasoning with exact probability (including probabilistic correctness and probabilistic termination).
The examples of Grover’s fixed-point search and recursive quantum Fourier sampling
are used to illustrate rules for proving partial and total correctness
of recursive quantum programs with deterministic control (containing auxiliary facilities).
We compare our quantum Hoare logic with other (deterministic, probabilistic or quantum) Hoare-like logics,
and discuss local and global reasoning in the setting of quantum computing.

1.3.
Organization of the Paper

We present preliminaries on quantum program verification in Sec.
2
;
syntax and semantics of recursive quantum programs are defined in Sec.
3
;
quantum assertion logic is presented in Sec.
4
;
starting proof systems are shown in Sec.
5
;
expanded proof systems including auxiliary facilities are shown in Sec.
6
;
Case studies of Grover’s fixed-point search and recursive quantum Fourier sampling are presented in Sec.
7
;
Comparison with the related work is given in Sec.
8
;
Sec.
9
concludes the paper with a discussion of the future work.

The running example of recursive quantum Markov chain is throughout the paper from Subsec.
3.1

through Subsecs.
3.2
and
3.3
to Subsec.
5.3
.
The proof (or proof schetch) of various results,
including proof for the two counterexamples — Exms.
5.1
and
5.2
,
proof of soundness and completeness results,
and a full verification of Grover’s fixed-point search and recursive quantum Fourier sampling,
are put into the appendix.

2.
Preliminaries

For convenience of the reader,
we briefly review the basics of quantum theory,
and fix the symbols and notations used in the subsequent sections.

2.1.
Quantum states

Definition of linear operators

The state space of a quantum system is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
For any positive integer
n
,
an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space is essentially the space
\mathbb{C}^{n}
of complex vectors.
We use Dirac’s notation,
|\psi\rangle
, to denote a complex vector in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
The inner product (resp. outer product) of two vectors
|\psi\rangle
and
|\phi\rangle
,
denoted
\langle\psi|\phi\rangle
(resp.
|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|
),
is the product of
\langle\psi|\triangleq(|\psi\rangle)^{{\dagger}}
(i.e. the conjugate transpose of
|\psi\rangle
)
and
|\phi\rangle
(resp. the product of
|\psi\rangle
and
\langle\phi|
).
The norm of a vector
|\psi\rangle
is denoted by
||\psi||\triangleq\sqrt{\langle\psi|\psi\rangle}
.
We say that a set of vectors
\{|\psi_{i}\rangle\}_{i}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
,
if
\langle\psi_{i}|\psi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j
(where
\delta_{i,j}=1
if
i=j
, and
=0
otherwise).
Then every vector of
\mathcal{H}
can be represented as a linear combination of any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
.
We define (linear) operators over
\mathcal{H}
as a linear mapping.
In the space
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, an operator
A
is represented by an
n\times n
matrix.
We say that
A
is Hermitian, if
A=A^{\dagger}
(where
A^{\dagger}
denotes the conjugate transpose of
A
).
Let
I_{\mathcal{H}}
(resp.
0_{\mathcal{H}}
) be the identity (resp. zero) operator over
\mathcal{H}
.
The trace of an operator
A
is defined by
\mathit{tr}(A)\triangleq\Sigma_{i}\langle i|A|i\rangle

(the sum of entries on the main diagonal of
A
w.r.t. any orthonormal basis of
A
).

Lemma 2.0

(Spectral decomposition, cf.
(
Nielsen and
Chuang 2000
, Box 2.2)
)
.

Every linear operator
A
of Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is Hermitian,
if, and only if, it can be decomposed as
A=\sum p_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|
,
where
\{p_{i}\}_{i}
are reals and
\{|\psi_{i}\rangle\}_{i}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
.

Löwner order between linear operators

An operator
A
is positive, if for all vectors
|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
,
\langle\psi|A|\psi\rangle\geq 0
.
Note that every positive operator is Hermitian,
and that every Hermitian operator of the form
\sum p_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|

is positive iff
p_{i}\geq 0,\forall i
(cf. Lem.
2.1
).
The concept of positivity induces the Löwner order
\sqsubseteq
between operators:

•

A\sqsubseteq B
, if
B-A
is positive;

•

A=B
, if
A\sqsubseteq B
and
B\sqsubseteq A
.

By definition, it follows, for any operators
A
and
B
, that

•

0_{\mathcal{H}}\sqsubseteq A
iff
A
is positive;

•

A\sqsubseteq B
iff there is a positive operator
C
s.t.
A+C=B
.

The least upper bound L. U. B. (resp. greatest lower bound G. L. B.) operator in a complete partial order
generated by Löwner comparison is denoted as
\bigsqcup
(resp.
\bigsqcap
).
For example, the L. U. B. (resp. G. L. B.) of a sequence of operators
\{A_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}

with
\forall n\geq 0.\ A_{n}\sqsubseteq A_{n+1}
(resp.
\forall n\geq 0.\ A_{n}\sqsupseteq A_{n+1}
)
will be denoted by
\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}A_{n}
(resp.
\bigsqcap_{n\geq 0}A_{n}
).
For the existence of those bounds, the reader is referred to the literature
(
Selinger 2004a
)
and
(
Ying 2016
)
.

Pure quantum state

A pure quantum state is represented by a unit vector,
i.e., a vector
|\psi\rangle
with
||\psi||=1

(used to represent the data states of a quantum circuit).
For example, a qubit, or quantum bit, system refers to the case when
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
An important basis of a qubit system is the computational basis
with
|0\rangle\triangleq(1,0)^{\dagger}
and
|1\rangle\triangleq(0,1)^{\dagger}
,
which corresponds to the
0/1
in a classical bit.
Another important basis, called the
\pm
basis,
consists of
|\pm\rangle\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle\pm|1\rangle)
.
One can represent multi-qubits by tensor-producting each qubit.
For instance, the classical two-bit string
01
can be represented by
|0\rangle\otimes|1\rangle
(or
|01\rangle
for short).
An
m
-qubit system lives in the space
\mathbb{C}^{2^{m}}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes m}
that is the
m
-time tensor product of a single qubit system
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Mixed quantum state

However, after applying a quantum measurement,
a (pure) quantum state is possibly changed to a mixed state,
i.e. a random distribution over an ensemble of pure states
\mathbb{E}=\{(p_{i},|\psi_{i}\rangle)\}_{i}
,
which states that the system is in state
|\psi_{i}\rangle
with probability
p_{i}
.
One can also use density operators to represent both pure and mixed quantum states.
Formally, a density operator is a positive operator
\rho
whose trace
\mathit{tr}(\rho)=1
.
For example, the density operator
\rho
for a mixed state represented by the
ensemble
\mathbb{E}
is
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|
; in particular,
a pure state
|\psi\rangle
can be identified with the density operator
\rho=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
.

Representation of quantum states

If density operators are used directly to represent quantum states,
we will find that the resulting state after applying a quantum operation
is probably not a density operator but its sub-part.
Note that the missing part of the final state is due to non-termination.
To cope with this issue, the concept of partial density operator (abbr.
\mathrm{PDO}
)
is introduced by Selinger
(
Selinger 2004b
)
to model a sub-part of a density operator.
Strictly speaking, a
\mathrm{PDO}
is a positive operator
\rho
with
0\leq\mathit{tr}(\rho)\leq 1

(Particularly, a density operator is a
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho
with
\mathit{tr}(\rho)=1
).
Defining quantum states as
\mathrm{PDO}
s ensures that quantum states are closed under quantum operations.
The set of
\mathrm{PDO}
s on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
.

2.2.
Quantum operations

Definition of quantum operations

The evolution of an open quantum system
\mathcal{H}

can be characterized by an (admissible) quantum operation (abbr.
\mathrm{QOP}
)
\mathcal{E}
,
which is a linear, trace-non-increasing and completely positive super operator from
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})

(By complete positivity of
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{H}
is meant that
for all linear operators
A
on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{H}
with
A\sqsupseteq 0_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{E}(A)\sqsupseteq 0_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}
).
Namely, for any state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
,
the final state after the
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}
is
\mathcal{E}(\rho)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
with
\mathit{tr}(\mathcal{E}(\rho))\leq\mathit{tr}(\rho)
.
For every
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}
,
there exists a set of Kraus operators
\{E_{k}\}_{k}
s.t.

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{k}E_{k}\rho E_{k}^{\dagger},\forall\rho

(See, e.g.,
(
Nielsen and
Chuang 2000
)
).
We denote the Kraus form of
\mathcal{E}
by writing
\mathcal{E}=\sum_{k}E_{k}\diamond E_{k}^{\dagger}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is positive and trace-non-increasing, it holds that
0\sqsubseteq\sum_{k}E_{k}^{\dagger}E_{k}\sqsubseteq I
.
For example, an identity (resp. zero) operation refers to
I_{\mathcal{H}}\diamond I_{\mathcal{H}}
(resp.
0_{\mathcal{H}}\diamond 0_{\mathcal{H}}
).

Dual of quantum operation

The Schrödinger-Heisenberg dual of a
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}
, denoted
\mathcal{E}^{*}
, is defined as

•

\mathit{tr}\big(A\mathcal{E}(\rho)\big)=\mathit{tr}\big(\mathcal{E}^{*}(A)\rho\big)
,
\forall\rho
and
\forall A
;

•

or, in the Kraus form,
\mathcal{E}^{*}\triangleq\sum_{k}E_{k}^{\dagger}\diamond E_{k}
, if
\mathcal{E}=\sum_{k}E_{k}\diamond E_{k}^{\dagger}
.

Lemma 2.0
.

Let
\lambda\geq 0
,
let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be respective
\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
let
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
be
\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\mathcal{H}
,
and let
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}
be a non-decreasing sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\mathcal{H}
.
It is the case that

(1):

(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})^{*}=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}^{*}
;

(2):

(\lambda\mathcal{F})^{*}=\lambda\mathcal{F}^{*}
;

(3):

(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G})^{*}=\mathcal{F}^{*}+\mathcal{G}^{*}
;

(4):

(\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G})^{*}=\mathcal{G}^{*}\circ\mathcal{F}^{*}
;

(5):

(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n})^{*}=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}
.

Representation of unitary operators

Operations (or evolutions) on (closed) quantum systems can be characterized by a unitary operator.
An operator
U
is a unitary operator if its conjugate transpose is its own inverse, i.e.,
U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}=I
.
Common single-qubit unitary operators include the Hadamard operator
H
and the Pauli operator
X
:

H\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
1&-1\end{array}\right],\quad X\triangleq\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right]

Intuitively,
H
transforms between the computational and the
\pm
basis,
i.e.,
H|0\rangle=|+\rangle
and
H|1\rangle=|-\rangle
,
and
X
is a bit flip, i.e.,
X|0\rangle=|1\rangle
and
X|1\rangle=|0\rangle
.
The evolution of
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho
under unitary operator
U
is
\rho\rightarrow U\rho U^{\dagger}
,
and thus
U
can be written as a
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}=U\diamond U^{\dagger}
.

Representation of measurement

The way to extract information about a quantum system is called a quantum measurement.
Mathematically, a quantum measurement on a system over
\mathcal{H}

can be described by a set of linear operators
\{M_{m}\}_{m}
with
\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}M_{m}=I_{\mathcal{H}}
.
If we perform a measurement
\{M_{m}\}_{m}
on a state
\rho
,
the outcome
m
is observed with probability
p_{m}\triangleq\mathit{tr}(M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger})
for each
m
,
and, with the observation
m
, the state collapses to a post-measurement state
M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger}/p_{m}
.
To characterize the evolution of a quantum measurement as a
\mathrm{QOP}
,
we remark that the probability
p_{m}
can be encoded into the post-measurement state
M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger}/p_{m}
,
resulting in
M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger}
.
So, such an evolution can be written as the
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}=M_{m}\diamond M_{m}^{\dagger}
.
One of the major differences between classical and quantum computation
is that a quantum measurement could potentially change the state itself.
For example, if we perform the standard (i.e. computational-basis) measurement

M=\{M_{0}\triangleq|0\rangle\langle 0|,M_{1}\triangleq|1\rangle\langle 1|\}
on state
\rho=|+\rangle\langle+|
,
then with probability
\frac{1}{2}
the outcome is
0
(resp.
1
),
and the final state becomes
|0\rangle\langle 0|
(resp.
|1\rangle\langle 1|
).

Representation of open systems

Suppose that we have a joint quantum system
\mathcal{H}\triangleq Q\otimes R

and wish to trace out system
R
by using partial trace function
\mathit{tr}_{R}
.
Let
\{|i\rangle\}_{i}
be an orthonormal basis for
R
.
Then
\mathit{tr}_{R}
can be defined by the
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathit{tr}_{R}\triangleq\sum_{i}\langle i|\diamond|i\rangle
.
We can represent a (separable) joint
\mathrm{QOP}
for quantum systems
Q
and
R

by the tensor product
\mathcal{E}_{Q}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{R}
of
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\mathcal{E}_{Q}
for
Q
and
\mathcal{E}_{R}
for
R
.
It is a convention in the quantum information literature that
when operations only apply to part of a quantum system,
one should assume that an identity operation is applied on the rest.
For example, applying
\mathcal{E}_{R}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{Q}
)
to
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(Q\otimes R)
means applying
(I_{Q}\diamond I_{Q})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{R}

[resp.
\mathcal{E}_{Q}\otimes(I_{R}\diamond I_{R})
] to
\rho
.
Here the identity operation is usually omitted for simplicity.
Thus, writing
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
with
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})

will naturally imply that
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Löwner order between super operators

Define the Löwner order between
\mathrm{QOP}
s based on that between linear operators:

•

\mathcal{E}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}
, if
\mathcal{E}(\rho)\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}(\rho)
,
\forall\rho
;

•

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
, if
\mathcal{E}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}
.

By definition, we have, for any
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
, that

•

0\diamond 0\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}
iff
\mathcal{E}
is completely positive;

•

\mathcal{E}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}
iff there is
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{G}
s.t.
\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}
.

The least upper bound
\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}
(resp. greatest lower bound
\bigsqcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}
)
of a sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\{\mathcal{E}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
with
\forall n\geq 0.\ \mathcal{E}_{n}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}_{n+1}

(resp.
\forall n\geq 0.\ \mathcal{E}_{n}\sqsupseteq\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
) is defined as

•

\big(\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}\big)(\rho)\triangleq\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\rho)
,
\forall\rho
;

•

\big(\bigsqcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}\big)(\rho)\triangleq\bigsqcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\rho)
,
\forall\rho
.

2.3.
Quantum predicates

Definition of quantum predicates

As defined in
(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
)
,
a quantum predicate (abbr.
\mathrm{QPRD}
) on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a Hermitian operator
M_{\mathcal{H}}

such that
0_{\mathcal{H}}\sqsubseteq M_{\mathcal{H}}\sqsubseteq I_{\mathcal{H}}
.
The satisfiability of a state (i.e.
\mathrm{PDO}
)
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
in the
\mathrm{QPRD}

M_{\mathcal{H}}
with
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is defined by the trace
\mathit{tr}(M_{\mathcal{H}}\rho)
if
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
and
\mathit{tr}\big((M_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}})\rho\big)
if
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.
Intuitively,
\mathit{tr}(M_{\mathcal{H}}\rho)

[
resp.
\mathit{tr}\big((M_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}})\rho\big)
]

is the expectation of the truth value of predicate
M_{\mathcal{H}}
in state
\rho
.
Note that restricting
M_{\mathcal{H}}
to between
0_{\mathcal{H}}
and
I_{\mathcal{H}}

ensures that
0\leq\mathit{tr}(M_{\mathcal{H}}\rho)\leq 1

[
resp.
0\leq\mathit{tr}\big((M_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}})\rho\big)\leq 1
]

for any
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
.
We shall write
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for the set of
\mathrm{QPRD}
s on
\mathcal{H}
.

How to use quantum predicates

By Lem.
2.1
, every
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho
has the spectral decomposition

\rho\triangleq\sum_{i\in A}p_{i}|\varphi_{i}\rangle\langle\varphi_{i}|
,
where
\forall i\in A.\ 0\leq p_{i}\leq 1
,

\sum_{i\in A}p_{i}\leq 1
and
\sum_{i\in A}|\varphi_{i}\rangle\langle\varphi_{i}|=I
.
In practice, we prefer to use projection operators, e.g.

M\triangleq\sum_{i\in B}|\varphi_{i}\rangle\langle\varphi_{i}|
,
with
B\subseteq A
, to define the (precise) properties of
\rho
.
Intuitively speaking,

\mathit{tr}(M\rho)=\sum_{i\in B}p_{i}
is the probability of
\rho
falling into the subspace represented by
M
.
In particular,
\mathit{tr}(|\varphi_{i}\rangle\langle\varphi_{i}|\rho)=p_{i}
is the probability of
\rho
in the state
|\varphi_{i}\rangle\langle\varphi_{i}|
, which can be used to reveal internal ingredients of a quantum state;

\mathit{tr}(I\rho)=\mathit{tr}(\rho)
is the probability of
\rho
falling into the whole space,
which is one of the usual statistical properties on quantum states.
For example, the
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho\triangleq\frac{|0\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|}{2}

represents the resulting state
\{(\frac{1}{2},|0\rangle),(\frac{1}{2},|1\rangle)\}
after a standard measurement on
|+\rangle
. Note that
\mathit{tr}(|0\rangle\langle 0|\rho)=\frac{1}{2}
(resp.
\mathit{tr}(|1\rangle\langle 1|\rho)=\frac{1}{2}
) is the probability of
\rho
in the state
|0\rangle\langle 0|
(resp.
|1\rangle\langle 1|
),
\mathit{tr}(I\rho)=1
is the probability of
\rho
falling into the whole space.

Expressiveness of quantum predicates

\mathrm{QPRD}
s can be used to describe classical properties (at the propositional level).
For example, we can use a main diagonal matrix – a kind of
\mathrm{QPRD}
–
to express (probabilistic) boolean functions,
if classical information is encoded as (a random distribution of) states of a computational basis.
To sum up, the quantum counterpart of a classical predicate
is a main diagonal matrix that encodes its indicator function.
The strength of adopting
\mathrm{QPRD}
s as assertions, among many others,
lies in the fact that various properties of quantum effects can be represented thereof.
For example, the predicate
M=|+\rangle\langle+|
, i.e.

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|0\rangle\langle 0|+|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|}{2}

describes that a state
\rho
is in the equal superposition
|+\rangle
with probability
\mathit{tr}(M\rho)
;
the predicate
N=\frac{|00\rangle+|11\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\langle 00|+\langle 11|}{\sqrt{2}}
, i.e.

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|00\rangle\langle 00|+|00\rangle\langle 11|+|11\rangle\langle 00|+|11\rangle\langle 11|}{2}

describes that a state
\rho
is in the maximally entangled state
\frac{|00\rangle+|11\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}
with probability
\mathit{tr}(N\rho)
.

Definition of quantum implication

The Löwner comparison
M\sqsubseteq N
between
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
M
and
N

is a quantum simulation of the classical implication “
F\rightarrow G
”.
Recall that the validity of
F\rightarrow G
, denoted
\models F\rightarrow G
,
is defined as: for any assignment
v
,
v\models F\implies v\models G
,
where
v\models F
denotes the satisfiability (truth value) of
F
under
v
.
The following lemma extends the semantics of classical implication into the quantum case

(
To better see this, we remark that
the less than or equal to
\leq
defined on the closed real interval
[0,1]

can be seen as a probabilistic extension of the classical implication
\implies
on the set of truth values
\{0,1\}
)
.

Lemma 2.0

(A semantic viewpoint of Löwner order
(
Ying 2011
, Lem. 2.1)
)
.

Let
M,N\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
. Then
M\sqsubseteq N
if,
and only if,
\mathit{tr}(M\rho)\leq\mathit{tr}(N\rho)
for all
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
.

3.
Recursive quantum programs

Recursive quantum programs can be viewed
as a recursive procedural extension of quantum base language
\mathit{qPL}

— the non-while-loop part of quantum
\mathbf{while}
-language introduced in
(
Ying 2011
;
Ying 2016
)
.
In this section, we define its syntax and formal semantics,
and introduce the example of recursive quantum Markov chain as the running example of the paper.

3.1.
Definition of the syntax

We assume a set
\mathit{Var}
of quantum variables annotated with a type
\mathbf{Bool}
or
\mathbf{Int}
,
and
\bar{q}\triangleq(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})\subseteq\mathit{Var}
.
For each
q\in\mathit{Var}
, its state Hilbert space is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{q}
.
Then
\bar{q}
is associated with the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}\triangleq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}_{q_{i}}
.
If
\it type(q)=\mathbf{Bool}
,
then
\mathcal{H}_{q}
is a two-dimensional Hilbert space with computational basis
\{|0\rangle,|1\rangle\}

(i.e. the space of a qubit).
If
\it type(q)=\mathbf{Int}
,
then
\mathcal{H}_{q}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space with computational basis
\{|n\rangle\colon n\in\mathbb{Z}\}

(i.e. the space of a quint, e.g. the space of a photon).
Note that we are able to use multiple (e.g.
n
) qubits to form any finite (e.g.
2^{n}
) dimensional Hilbert space,
and usually use the computational basis of an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space to simulate integers
\mathbb{Z}
,
which can be used to implement the classical (deterministic) control of a quantum program (cf. Exm.
5.1
).
(Working with infinite Hilbert space is as with finite space, see, e.g.,
(
Prugovecki 1982
)
.)

Now we are able to define a (possibly recursive) procedural extension
of quantum base language
\mathit{qPL}

(the non-while-loop part of quantum
\mathbf{while}
-language
(
Ying 2011
;
Ying 2016
)
),
denoted by
\mathit{RqPL}
(We can implement a while loop as a tail recursion, cf. Subsec.
5.4
).
A recursive quantum program
P\in\mathit{RqPL}
usually consists of a procedural declaration
D
,
associating some body with a procedure name,
followed by some statement
S
possibly containing activation statements to declared procedures.
Formally,
\mathit{RqPL}
is generated by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}P&\triangleq&D::S&\mbox{Quantum program}\\
D&\triangleq&\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{1}\rangle\colon S_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{n}\rangle\colon S_{n}&\mbox{Procedure declaration}\\
S&\triangleq&\mathbf{bot}&\mbox{Bottom}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{skip}&\mbox{No operation}\\
&\mid&q:=|0\rangle&\mbox{Initialization}\\
&\mid&\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U&\mbox{Unitary operation}\\
&\mid&S_{1};S_{2}&\mbox{Sequential composition}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}&\mbox{Probabilistic branching}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{call}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{i}\rangle,\quad 1\leq i\leq n&\mbox{Procedure call}\end{array}

where for each declared (possibly recursive) procedure

\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{i}\rangle\colon S_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,

\mathit{proc}_{i}
and
S_{i}
are the name and body of the procedure.
For the follow-up development,
we first assume that quantum programs have no local variables,
and that procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
dispense with parameter passing.
The treatment of these auxiliary facilities is deferred to Sec.
6
.

The intended semantics of language constructs above is similar to that of their classical counterparts.
To see the quantum features of those constructs,
we remark that:

(i):

for the initialization, the choice of the state
|0\rangle
as the initial value is due to the fact that any known quantum state can be prepared by applying a unitary operator to
|0\rangle
;

(ii):

for the probabilistic branching, different branches
\{S_{m}\}_{m}
are chosen
according to (randomly distributed) outcomes of the measurement
M\triangleq\{M_{m}\}_{m}
on the qubits
\bar{q}
,
and the measurement process could possibly destroy the current state.

Example 3.0 (Alternative definition of
\mathbf{bot}
).

Quantum program
\mathbf{bot}
, as a basic program construct, can also be implemented
as the call statement
\mathbf{call}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}
with
P_{\mathbf{bot}}
declared by

\mathbf{Proc}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}\colon\mathbf{call}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}

Remark 3.1.

For the syntax of
\mathit{RqPL}
,
we choose
\mathbf{bot}
as a basic program construct, rather than as in Exm.
3.1
,
because the denotational semantics of recursive procedures
and its derivatives (e.g. formal weakest preconditions)
will need to be defined on the “absolute” semantics of
\mathbf{bot}
as a meta-symbol,
otherwise a circular reasoning will occur.

Running example of the paper.

To illustrate that our method has the capacity for dealing with probabilistic control,
we adopt the example of recursive quantum Markov chains (abbr.
\mathrm{RQMC}
)
(
Feng
et al. 2013b
)

as the running example of the paper.

Example 3.0 (Syntax of
\mathrm{RQMC}
).

Let us introduce a modified version of Exm. 1 in the literature
(
Feng
et al. 2013b
)
.
This is a two-player (Alice and Bob) game of first tossing a dice,
simulated by a qubit system
q
,
and then making a decision for either being the final winner,
flagged as
|\pm\rangle
, or transferring
q
to the other.
The protocol of Alice goes as follows.
She first measures
q
immutably by the observable

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{M_{0}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}}I,\ M_{1}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}I,\ M_{2}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}}I\bigg\}

If the outcome
0
is observed, then she sets
q
to be
|+\rangle
and terminates;
if
1
is observed, then she transfers
q
to the Bob and lets him play;
if
2
is observed, the game will get stuck.
The protocol of Bob goes similarly except that
he will use the following observable

\displaystyle M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{M_{0}^{\prime}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}I,\ M_{1}^{\prime}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}I\bigg\}

instead, and if the measurement outcome
0
is observed, he sets
q
to be
|-\rangle
and terminates.
After Bob wins, Alice will do nothing and terminate immediately.
The game starts with Alice.
The core of the game is mutually recursive procedures
\mathit{Alice}
and
\mathit{Bob}
programmed as

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{Alice}\colon\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}\\
\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{Bob}\colon\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M^{\prime}[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}^{\prime}\ \mathbf{fi}\end{array}

where
\{S_{m}\}_{m=0,1,2}
and
\{S_{m}^{\prime}\}_{m=0,1}
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{lll}S_{0}\triangleq q\;{\ast}{=}\;H&S_{1}\triangleq\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}&S_{2}\triangleq\mathbf{bot}\\
S_{0}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}&S_{1}^{\prime}\triangleq q\;{\ast}{=}\;HX&\end{array}

The main program
\mathrm{RQMC}
of the game is as follows.

\displaystyle\mathrm{RQMC}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle q:=0;\ \mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}

3.2.
Nondeterministic operational semantics

(Bot)

\langle\mathbf{bot},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,0\rangle

(Skip)

\langle\mathbf{skip},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,\rho\rangle

(Init)

\dfrac{\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|=I_{q}}{\langle q:=|0\rangle,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,\sum_{i}|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|\rho|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle}

(Unit)

\dfrac{UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=I_{\bar{q}}}{\langle\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,U\rho U^{\dagger}\rangle}

(Comp
1
)

\dfrac{\langle S_{1},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{1}^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\rangle\mbox{ and }S_{1}^{\prime}\neq E}{\langle S_{1};S_{2},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{1}^{\prime};S_{2},\rho^{\prime}\rangle}

(Comp
2
)

\dfrac{\langle S_{1},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}{\langle S_{1};S_{2},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{2},\rho^{\prime}\rangle}

(Case)

\dfrac{M=\{M_{m}\}_{m}\mbox{ and }\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}M_{m}=I_{\bar{q}}}{\langle\mathbf{if},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{m}\langle S_{m},M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger}\rangle}

(Proc)

\dfrac{\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{proc}\colon S\in D}{\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle S,\rho\rangle}

(Except)

\dfrac{\mbox{Other cases of $S$ and $l$}}{\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\bot}

Table 1
.
Labeled transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}
with
l\triangleq\epsilon\mid m
.

The operational semantics of quantum programs can be defined as
a nondeterministic transition relation
\rightarrow

between quantum configurations
\langle S,\rho\rangle

— a global description for quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}

on the current state represented as a
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho
.
For the well-definedness of
\langle S,\rho\rangle
,
it is required that
\mathit{Var}(S)\subseteq\mathit{Var}(\rho)
.
Note that
S
could be the empty statement
E
indicating that
\rho
is the final output.
By a labeled transition

\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\rangle

we mean that program
S
on input state
\rho
is evaluated
in one step with label
l
to program
S^{\prime}
with output state
\rho^{\prime}
.
The transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}
for
\mathit{RqPL}
is defined in Tab.
1
.

To better understand the relation
\xrightarrow{l}
, we remark that

•

The transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}
is defined in a nondeterministic manner.
The unique source of nondeterminism is execution of a case statement,
and each measurement outcome
m
corresponds to a different path of execution.
For those deterministic one-step executions, we use
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}
instead
(
\epsilon
means there are no other choices).
Otherwise (when the cases of
S
and
l
mismatch),
the execution will fail and fall into a bottom state
\bot
.

•

The annotated outer product
|\psi\rangle_{\bar{q}}\langle\phi|
and identity operator
I_{\bar{q}}

mean that
|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|,I\in\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}
.

•

Here, and in the sequel,
the statement
\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}
is shortened with
\mathbf{if}
.

Example 3.0 (Operational semantics of
\mathrm{RQMC}
).

Let
\mathrm{RQMC}
be the quantum program defined in Exm.
3.2
.
Let annotated
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho_{q}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
with
\mathit{tr}(\rho_{q})=1
.
Then part of the operational semantics of
\mathrm{RQMC}
(Alice wins after two rounds)
can be developed step by step as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\langle q:=0;\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice},\rho_{q}\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice},|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi},|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{1}&\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob},\frac{1}{2}|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M^{\prime}[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}^{\prime}\ \mathbf{fi},\frac{1}{2}|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{0}&\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice},\frac{1}{4}|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi},\frac{1}{4}|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{0}&\langle q\;{\ast}{=}\;H,\frac{1}{16}|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle E,\frac{1}{16}|+\rangle_{q}\langle+|\rangle\end{array}

The last configuration shows that Alice wins after two rounds with probability
\frac{1}{16}
.

To extend the one-step labeled transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}

to a multi-step labeled transition relation
\xrightarrow{\alpha}

with
\alpha\triangleq l\mid\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
,
we define
\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}C

(
C
is either
\langle S^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\rangle
or
\bot
) as

\xrightarrow{l}
, defined in Tab.
1
, if
\alpha=l
;
or as
\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\langle S^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle

and
\langle S^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}C

for some
S^{\prime\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime\prime}
otherwise.

Remark 3.2 (Comparison with Ying’s operational semantics).

Ying’s original operational semantics
(
Ying 2011
;
Ying 2016
)
is
defined in a nondeterministic way without resorting to the concept of labels,
and there is only one rule (followed by the side condition
E;S_{2}\triangleq S_{2}
) for the case of composition,
which is able to combine together the (Comp
1
, Comp
2
) rules of Tab.
1
.
In other words, in order to define a more fine-grained operational semantics,
we have to introduce the concept of labeled transition relation,
at the price of introducing an extra rule (Except) dealing with the case of exception.

3.3.
\mathrm{QOP}
-directed denotational semantics

(Param)

\llbracket\Omega_{\bar{q}}\rrbracket=\omega_{\bar{q}}

(Bot)

\llbracket\mathbf{bot}\rrbracket=0\diamond 0

(Skip)

\llbracket\mathbf{skip}\rrbracket=I\diamond I

(Init)

\llbracket q:=|0\rangle\rrbracket=\sum_{i}|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|\diamond|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|

(Unit)

\llbracket\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U\rrbracket=U\diamond U^{\dagger}

(Comp)

\llbracket S_{1};S_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket

(Case)

\llbracket\mathbf{if}\rrbracket=\sum_{m}\llbracket S_{m}\rrbracket\circ(M_{m}\diamond M_{m}^{\dagger})

(Proc)

\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\rrbracket=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}\rrbracket

Table 2
.
Denotational semantics of
\mathit{RqPL}[\Omega]
.

The denotational semantics of a quantum program, denoted
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
, is defined as a super operator (i.e.
\mathrm{QOP}
).
The semantics of each term is given in a compositional way, except for the case of call statements.
To handle this case,
we need to define the syntactic approximation (i.e., unrolling) of the bodies of mutually recursive procedures.

Definition 3.0 (Syntactic approximation).

For declared recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
with body
S_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
the
k
th syntactic approximation
S_{i}^{(k)}
is defined as:

\displaystyle S_{i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathbf{bot}

\displaystyle S_{i}^{(k+1)}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle S_{i}\big[\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j},\ldots\big]

where
S_{i}\big[\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j},\ldots\big]

stands for simultaneous substitution of
\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}
for
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}
,
for all
1\leq j\leq n
, occurring inside
S_{i}

(
Here
\mathbf{skip}
is used to simulate the first-step transition
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}
for the statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}
,
cf. the (Skip, Proc) rules of Tab.
1
)
.

The denotational semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
for
\mathit{RqPL}
with parameters
\Omega

(denoted
\mathit{RqPL}[\Omega]
) is defined in Tab.
2
,
where the quantum program parameter
\Omega_{\bar{q}}
,
ranging over the set of all quantum programs for quantum variables
\bar{q}
,
is interpreted as the corresponding quantum operation parameter
\omega_{\bar{q}}
,
ranging over the set of all
\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}
.
This is justified by the fact that any
\mathrm{QOP}
can be simulated by a non-parameterized quantum program.

Lemma 3.0

(Well-definedness of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
)
.

Let
S_{i}^{\prime}
be a parameterized adaptation of
S_{i}
, the body of
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}
, with
1\leq i\leq n
,
by substituting parameterized quantum program
\mathbf{skip};\Omega_{j}
for each call-statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}

with
1\leq j\leq n
occurring inside
S_{i}
:

\displaystyle S_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle S_{i}\big[\big(\mathbf{skip};\Omega_{1}\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{1},\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};\Omega_{j}\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j},\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};\Omega_{n}\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{n}]

Let
\mathcal{F}(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n})
be a vectorial function on
\mathrm{QOP}
s defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\big(\llbracket S_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket,\ldots,\llbracket S_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket,\ldots,\llbracket S_{n}^{\prime}\rrbracket\big)

Define the least sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\big\{\mathcal{F}_{i}^{k}(\bar{0})\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(k)}\big\}_{k\geq 0}
,
0\leq i\leq n
, generated by
\mathcal{F}
as follows.

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{(0)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(0)}\big)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\big(0,\ldots,0,\ldots,0\big)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(k+1)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{(k+1)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(k+1)}\big)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{(k)},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(k)}\big)

It is the case that

(i):

\llbracket S_{i}^{(k)}\rrbracket=\mathcal{F}_{i}^{k}(\bar{0})
for all
k\geq 0
;

(ii):

\llbracket S_{i}^{(k)}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket S_{i}^{(k+1)}\rrbracket
for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

By definition of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
and
\big\{\mathcal{F}_{i}^{k}(\bar{0})\big\}_{k\geq 0}
, Stat. (i) follows.
To show Stat. (ii), we remark that
\mathcal{F}
is monotone,
i.e. for any
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\mathcal{E}_{j}
and
\mathcal{F}_{j}
with
\mathcal{E}_{j}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}_{j}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j},\ldots)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{j},\ldots)

(Note that
\sqsubseteq
distributes over components of the vector.)
This is the case due to linearity of (super operators)
\mathcal{F}
,
together with the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{j}+\mathcal{G}_{j}=\mathcal{F}_{j}
for some
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{G}_{j}
.
Then Stat. (ii) follows by induction on
k
, together with monotonicity of
\mathcal{F}
.
∎

Remark 3.3 (Comparison with Ying’s denotational semantics).

The denotational semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
of Table
2
is
defined as a composition of
\mathrm{QOP}
s independent of the input
\rho
,
which can be seen as a parameterized extension of
Ying’s original denotational semantics
(
Ying 2011
;
Ying 2016
)
.
The parameterized version of denotational semantics helps to explain that

•

\big\{\llbracket S_{i}^{(k)}\rrbracket\big\}_{k\geq 0}
,
0\leq i\leq n
, is the least sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s generated by
\mathcal{F}
;

•

\big\{\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\rrbracket\big\}_{0\leq i\leq n}
is the least fixed point of the vectorial function
\mathcal{F}
, i.e.

–

(\ldots,\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}\rrbracket,\ldots)=\mathcal{F}(\ldots,\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}\rrbracket,\ldots)
, and

–

for any
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}
with
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{j},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n})
, we have that
\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}_{j}
for all
0\leq j\leq n
.

as implied by Lem.
3.5
.

Example 3.0 (Denotational semantics of
\mathrm{RQMC}
).

Let
\mathrm{RQMC}
be the quantum program defined in Exm.
3.2
.
Let
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
denote the bodies of procedures
\mathit{Alice}
and
\mathit{Bob}
.
Then
S_{a}^{\prime}\triangleq S_{a}[\Omega_{b}/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}]
and
S_{b}^{\prime}\triangleq S_{b}[\Omega_{a}/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}]
.
Thus we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle(\llbracket S_{a}^{\prime}\rrbracket,\llbracket S_{b}^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(\frac{1}{4}H\diamond H+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{b},\frac{1}{2}\omega_{a}+\frac{1}{2}HX\diamond XH)

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}^{n}(\bar{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k-1\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)H\diamond H+\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)HX\diamond XH

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}^{n}(\bar{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k\leq n}\frac{1}{2\cdot 4^{k}}\Big)H\diamond H+\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k-1\leq n}\frac{1}{2\cdot 4^{k-1}}\Big)HX\diamond XH

By the (Proc) rule of Tab.
2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{1}^{n}(\bar{0})=\frac{1}{3}H\diamond H+\frac{1}{3}HX\diamond XH

Finally, for any
\mathrm{PDO}

\rho
with
\mathit{tr}(\rho)=1
, we have that

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathrm{RQMC}\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket q:=0;\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket q:=0\rrbracket)(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\rrbracket\big(\llbracket q:=0\rrbracket(\rho)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\rrbracket(|0\rangle\langle 0|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}H|0\rangle\langle 0|H+\frac{1}{3}HX|0\rangle\langle 0|XH

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}|+\rangle\langle+|+\frac{1}{3}|-\rangle\langle-|=\frac{1}{3}I

Remark 3.4.

After round
n=2k-1
(
k\geq 1
) of the two-player game (cf. Exm.
3.3
),
there is a computed result
\frac{1}{4^{k}}|+\rangle\langle+|
,
but the resulting state as a whole (for all
k\geq 0
) should include the sum of all, i.e.,

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{4^{k}}|+\rangle\langle+|=\frac{1}{3}|+\rangle\langle+|
(cf. Exm.
3.6
).

The following Theorem reveals the connection between operational and denotational semantics.
Namely, the meaning of running program
S
on input state
\rho

is the sum of all possible output states
\rho^{\prime}

(
Note that
\mathit{tr}(\rho^{\prime})
denotes the probability of reaching
\rho^{\prime}/\mathit{tr}(\rho^{\prime})
)
.

Theorem 3.7
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
, we have that

(1)

\displaystyle\llbracket S\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}

where the summation of
\rho^{\prime}
is taken for every possible
\alpha
s.t.
\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof.

See the proof of Thm.
A.3
.
∎

Remark 3.5.

To the right-hand side of Eq. (
1
) in Thm.
3.7
,
the summation should act upon any (possibly overlapping)
\rho^{\prime}
,
as long as
\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle
holds for a different
\alpha
.
For instance, there are two branches of a program each having the computed result
\frac{1}{4}|0\rangle\langle 0|
,
then the resulting state as a whole should include the sum of both, i.e.,

\frac{1}{4}|0\rangle\langle 0|+\frac{1}{4}|0\rangle\langle 0|=\frac{1}{2}|0\rangle\langle 0|
.
By comparison, Ying’s original treatment of this issue
(
Ying 2011
;
Ying 2016
)
,
due to lack of labels in transition rules, is to use a multi-set instead to collect up all possible outputs.

4.
Quantum assertion logic

\mathrm{QPRD}
s are simply employed in
(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
;
Ying 2011
)

as pre- and post-conditions of quantum Hoare’s triples.
However, to achieve an effectively checkable (symbolic) Löwner comparison between parameterized quantum predicates,
we have to first define a parameterized symbolic abstraction for
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
— Parameterized Quantum Predicate Terms (abbr.
\mathrm{PQPT}
),
whose definition should be a trade off between simplicity and expressibility
so that we make a minimal use of parameters ranging over a continuous space, and at the same time,
make sure both
\mathrm{QPRD}
s and all (possibly parameterized) intermediate assertions,
e.g. weakest (liberal) preconditions, in quantum program verification can be expressed thereof.

\mathrm{QOP}
’s dual as predicate transformer

To see the role of a
\mathrm{QOP}
’s dual in defining its weakest precondition (i.e.
\mathrm{QPRD}
),
note that every
\mathrm{QOP}

\mathcal{E}
can be seen as a mapping over
\mathrm{PDO}
s
\rho

and its dual
\mathcal{E}^{*}
as a mapping over
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
M
, i.e.

\begin{array}[]{rlccc}\mathcal{E}&\colon&\rho&\mapsto&\mathcal{E}(\rho)\\
\mathcal{E}^{*}&\colon&\mathcal{E}^{*}(M)&\mapsfrom&M\end{array}

By definition of Schrödinger-Heisenberg dual, we have that

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}\big(\mathcal{E}^{*}(M)\rho\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}\big(M\mathcal{E}(\rho)\big)

Intuitively, the truth value of
\mathcal{E}^{*}(M)
at
\rho
is equal to the truth value of
M
at
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
.
That is that,
\mathcal{E}^{*}(M)
is the weakest precondition of
\mathcal{E}
w.r.t. the postcondition
M

(
D’Hondt and
Panangaden 2006
)
.
The beautiful duality between state-transformer (forwards) and predicate-transformer (backwards) semantics
plays a key role in defining quantum assertion logic using the predicate transform
\mathcal{E}^{*}
.

4.1.
Parameterized quantum predicate terms

Definition 4.0 (Syntax of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).

Let
\bar{q}
,
\bar{r}
and
\bar{s}
be lists of pairwise distinct quantum variables
with
\bar{q}=(\bar{r},\bar{s})
(if any).
Let
I_{\bar{q}}
be the constant symbol denoting the identity operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}
,

\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}
a metavariable for all first-order variables ranging over
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})

with
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}
(i.e. quantum predicate variables),
and
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}},\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}

\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}

with
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}\sqsubseteq I_{\bar{q}}\diamond I_{\bar{q}}
.

A parameterized base
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}
of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s on
\bar{q}

(
and its set of parameters
\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})
)
is defined as

\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}\ \big(\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})\big)\quad\triangleq\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}&\big(\{\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}\}\big)\\
I_{\bar{r}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}&\big(\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}})\big)\\
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}\otimes I_{\bar{s}}&\big(\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}})\big)\\
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}&\big(\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}})\uplus\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}})\big)\end{array}\right.

A
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{\bar{q}}
on
\bar{q}

(
and its set of parameters
\mathit{Prmt}(P_{\bar{q}})
)
is defined as

P_{\bar{q}}\ \big(\mathit{Prmt}(P_{\bar{q}})\big)\quad\triangleq\quad\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})\ \big(\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})\big)

Note that a
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{\bar{q}}
is usually composed of two parts:
the parameterized part
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})

and the non-parameterized part
\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})
,
which can also be written as

\displaystyle P_{\bar{q}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}^{*}\bigg(\bigotimes_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathit{Prmt}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}})}\mathcal{X}\bigg)+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})

due to the convention that the identity operators in
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}
can be omitted.

Semantics of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be the standard interpretation of nonlogical symbols
in the syntax of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s to the semantic counterparts.
Let
v
be a mapping (i.e. assignment) from variables
\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}
to
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})
,
i.e.
v(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})
.
The denotation of a
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{\bar{q}}
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
and assignment
v
, denoted
P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}
, can be defined as usual (cf., e.g., Def.
B.1
).

To see well-definedness of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s, i.e.
P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})
,
we note that
P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}
is Hermitian and

0_{\bar{q}}=0_{\bar{q}}\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}0_{\bar{q}}+0_{\bar{q}}I_{\bar{q}}0_{\bar{q}}\sqsubseteq P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}=\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v})+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})\sqsubseteq\big(\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}\big)^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})\sqsubseteq I_{\bar{q}}

The set of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s on
\bar{q}
is denoted
\mathcal{T}(\bar{q})
.
We shall write
P_{\bar{q}}
as
P
if
\bar{q}
is clear from the context.

Lemma 4.0

(From
\mathrm{QPRD}
s to
\mathrm{PQPT}
s)
.

For every
\mathrm{QPRD}

M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})
,
there is a
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{\bar{q}}
of the form

0_{\bar{q}}\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}0_{\bar{q}}+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})=\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})

such that
M=P_{\bar{q}}
.

Proof.

By Lem.
2.1
,

\mathrm{QPRD}

M
has the spectral decomposition
M=\sum_{k}a_{k}|\varphi_{k}\rangle\langle\varphi_{k}|
.
Then the lemma follows by defining
\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}
as

\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}\triangleq\sum_{k}\big(\sqrt{a_{k}}|\varphi_{k}\rangle\langle\varphi_{k}|\big)\diamond\big(\sqrt{a_{k}}|\varphi_{k}\rangle\langle\varphi_{k}|\big)^{\dagger}
.
∎

Remark 4.1.

The quantum tautology
I
, quantum absurdity
0
, quantum predicate variable
\mathcal{X}
,
and the negation
I-P
of
\mathrm{PQPT}

P
with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\emptyset

are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s of the form
0(\mathcal{B})0+I(I)I
,
0(\mathcal{B})0+0(I)0
,
I(\mathcal{X})I+0(I)0
,
and
\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})
(cf. Lem.
4.2
), respectively.

Definition 4.0 (Operations on
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).

Let
P
,
\{P_{m}\}_{m}
(resp.
Q_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq l
)
be
\mathrm{PQPT}
s on quantum variables
\bar{q}
(resp.
\bar{q}_{i}
s.t.
\biguplus_{1\leq i\leq l}\bar{q}_{i}
exists),

P_{\bar{r}},P_{\bar{s}}

\mathrm{PQPT}
s on
\bar{r},\bar{s}
,
and
\{\mathcal{E}_{m}\}_{m}

\mathrm{QOP}
s on
\bar{q}
with
\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}_{m}\sqsubseteq I_{\bar{q}}\diamond I_{\bar{q}}
.
Define operations on
\mathrm{PQPT}
s as follows.

(Substitution).:

P[Q_{1}/\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,Q_{i}/\mathcal{X}_{i},\ldots,Q_{l}/\mathcal{X}_{l}]
is the result of (simultaneously) substituting
Q_{i}
for the (at most one) occurrence of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
in
P
for all
1\leq i\leq l
, if

•:

|\mathit{Prmt}(P)|\leq 1
with
\big\{Q_{i}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}_{i}}^{*}(\mathcal{X}_{i})+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}_{i}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}_{i}})\big\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
, or

•:

|\mathit{Prmt}(P)|\geq 2
with
\big\{Q_{i}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}_{i}}^{*}(\mathcal{X}_{i})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}_{i}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}_{i}})\big\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
.

(Conjunction).:

P_{\bar{r}}\otimes P_{\bar{s}}
is the quantum conjunction of
P_{\bar{r}}
and
P_{\bar{s}}
, if

•:

P_{\bar{r}}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{\bar{r}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}})
and
P_{\bar{s}}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{\bar{s}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}})
, or

•:

\mathit{Prmt}(P_{\bar{r}})=\emptyset
or
\mathit{Prmt}(P_{\bar{s}})=\emptyset
.

(Disjunction).:

\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}_{m}^{*}(P_{m})
is the quantum disjunction of
\{P_{m}\}_{m}
under the exclusive case selection
\{\mathcal{E}_{m}\}_{m}
, if

•:

\big\{\mathit{Prmt}(P_{m})\big\}_{m}
is a singleton.

Lemma 4.0
.

\mathrm{PQPT}
s are closed under those operations defined in Def.
4.3
.

Proof.

By Def.
4.1
, together with Lem.
2.2
.
∎

Example 4.0 (Quantum predicate variables).

•

The
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{q}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{r}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{s}
can induce, by substitution for
\mathcal{X}_{r}
and
\mathcal{X}_{s}
, any
\mathrm{PQPT}
of the form
P_{q}\otimes Q_{r}\otimes R_{s}
(no entanglement between
r
and
s
).

•

P_{q}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{r,s}
can produce any
\mathrm{PQPT}
of the form
P_{q}\otimes Q_{r,s}
(where
Q_{r,s}
possibly expresses an entanglement between
r
and
s
).

Example 4.0 (Modeling classical predicates).

Let quantum variable
q
be such that
\mathcal{H}_{q}
has the computational basis
\{|i\rangle\}_{i}

(i.e.,
I_{q}=\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|
).
Then the
\mathrm{PQPT}

\displaystyle\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|i\rangle|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|

(By comparison,
\mathcal{X}_{q}
has the outer product representation

\mathcal{X}_{q}=\sum_{i,j}\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|j\rangle|i\rangle_{q}\langle j|
)
can be used to simulate a classical first-order variable
x

over the domain
\{(\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|i\rangle,i)\}_{i}
,
each element
i
occurring with probability
\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|i\rangle
.
Based on this, the classical parameterized predicate
\varphi(x)
can be simulated by

\sum_{i}\langle i|\mathcal{X}_{q}|i\rangle|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|\otimes\ulcorner\varphi(i)\urcorner
,
where
\ulcorner\varphi(i)\urcorner
is the
\mathrm{PQPT}
for simulating the closed predicate
\varphi(i)
.
For illustrating examples, see, e.g., case studies.

Remark 4.2 (Discretization).

Every
\mathrm{PQPT}
can be defined in a discrete space up to approximation.
To see this, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{QOP}
s can be discretized in the sense of approximation.
This is the case due to the fact that
any
\mathrm{QOP}
can be obtained by tracing out the environmental part of a global unitary operation,
and any unitary operation can be approximated to arbitrary accuracy
by a quantum circuit composed of a (fixed) finite set of gates,
e.g. Hadamard, CNOT and
\pi/8

(
Nielsen and
Chuang 2000
)
.

4.2.
Parameterized orders and limits

Parameterized order.

In our quantum program logic,
we shall use
\mathrm{PQPT}
s as pre- and post-conditions in place of
\mathrm{QPRD}
s.
In accordance with this change, the Löwner comparison between
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
will be replaced by a Löwner ordering formula for
\mathrm{PQPT}
s,
and quantum assertion theories will be redefined so as to provide these Löwner ordering formulas.

Definition 4.0 (Löwner order between
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).

Let
P
and
Q
be
\mathrm{PQPT}
s on quantum variables
\bar{q}
.
A (legitimate) Löwner ordering formula is of the form
P\sqsubseteq Q
or
P=Q
with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q)
,
and its formal semantics (i.e. truth value) is defined as follows.

•

\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq Q
, if
P^{\mathbb{I},v}\sqsubseteq Q^{\mathbb{I},v},\forall v
.

•

\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=Q
, if
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq Q
and
\models_{\mathbb{I}}Q\sqsubseteq P
.

Example 4.0.

We illustrate valid (parameterized) Löwner ordering formulas by two items.

•

\langle 0|\mathcal{X}|0\rangle|0\rangle\langle 0|\sqsubseteq\langle 0|\mathcal{X}|0\rangle|0\rangle\langle 0|+\langle 1|\mathcal{X}|1\rangle|1\rangle\langle 1|
;

•

X|0\rangle\langle 0|\mathcal{X}|0\rangle\langle 0|X=|+\rangle\langle 0|\mathcal{X}|0\rangle\langle+|
.

Definition 4.0 (Quantum assertion theories).

•

The set of true
\sqsubseteq
-ordered
\mathrm{PQPT}
s under
\mathbb{I}
, denoted
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}
, is defined as

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}\quad\triangleq\quad\bigcup_{\bar{q}}\big\{P\sqsubseteq Q\colon P,Q\in\mathcal{T}(\bar{q}),\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q),\textup{ and }\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq Q\big\}

•

The set of true
=
-ordered
\mathrm{PQPT}
s under
\mathbb{I}
, denoted
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}
, is defined as

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}\quad\triangleq\quad\bigcup_{\bar{q}}\big\{P=Q\colon P,Q\in\mathcal{T}(\bar{q}),\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q),\textup{ and }\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=Q\big\}

Note that
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}
provides all (true) formal assertions on
\mathrm{PQPT}
s needed in this paper;
in the sequel,
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}
, as a subset of
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}
, can be used to reason with exact probabilities.

Lemma 4.0
.

Let
P\triangleq\mathcal{C}^{*}(\mathcal{B})+\mathcal{D}^{*}(I)
and
Q\triangleq\mathcal{E}^{*}(\mathcal{B})+\mathcal{F}^{*}(I)

be
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with the same base
\mathcal{B}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}
or
\mathcal{C}\sqsupset\mathcal{E}
(i.e.
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{E}
are Löwner comparable).
Then we have that

(1):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq Q
if, and only if,

•:

\mathcal{C}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{D}^{*}(I)\sqsubseteq\mathcal{F}^{*}(I)
, or

•:

\mathcal{C}\sqsupset\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{C}^{*}(I)+\mathcal{D}^{*}(I)\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}^{*}(I)+\mathcal{F}^{*}(I)
.

(2):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=Q
if, and only if,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{D}^{*}(I)=\mathcal{F}^{*}(I)
.

Proof.

For the proof of Stat. (1),
\mathcal{B}
is taken to be
0
if
\mathcal{C}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}
, and
I
otherwise.
For the proof of Stat. (2), first take
\mathcal{B}
to be
0
; then use the fact that
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{E}
are Löwner comparable.
∎

Remark 4.3.

The decision of
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq Q
(resp.
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=Q
)
will have to resort to positivity (resp. equality) of super operators on separable states,
which is beyond the scope of the current paper.
However, as Lem.
4.10
entails,
a restricted semantics of
P\sqsubseteq Q
(resp.
P=Q
) independent of parameters
can be defined based on Löwner order of
\mathrm{QOP}
s and
\mathrm{QPRD}
s,
in case that
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{E}
are comparable.
In what follows,
we shall adopt the standard semantics of a Löwner ordering formula,
but the restricted semantics applies too.

Parameterized limits.

For the definition of necessary intermediate assertions in verifying recursive procedures,
we need to introduce the concept of the L. U. B. (i.e. upper limit)
and G. L. B. (i.e. lower limit) of an infinite sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s.

Definition 4.0 (The upper and lower limits).

Let
I
and
P
be
\mathrm{PQPT}
s,
\mathcal{E}
and
\{\mathcal{E}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}

\mathrm{QOP}
s
with
\forall n\geq 0.\ \mathcal{E}_{n}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{E}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{n}

(For the existence of
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{n}
, cf. Sec.
2
).

•

Define the upper limit
\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}
of the sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\big\{P_{n}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{n}^{*}(P)\big\}_{n\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}P_{n}=\mathcal{E}^{*}(P)

•

Define the lower limit
\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{n}
of the sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\big\{Q_{n}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{n}^{*}(P)+\big(I-\mathcal{E}_{n}^{*}(I)\big)\big\}_{n\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}Q_{n}=\mathcal{E}^{*}(P)+\big(I-\mathcal{E}^{*}(I)\big)

Lemma 4.0

(Well-definedness of the limits)
.

Let
\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
and
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be as in Def.
4.11
.
Then we have that

•

\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P_{n}\sqsubseteq P_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
(thus denoted
\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
);

•

\{Q_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\models_{\mathbb{I}}Q_{n}\sqsupseteq Q_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
(thus denoted
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsupseteq}
).

Proof.

By Lem.
4.4
(closure of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s under disjunction), together with Lem.
4.10
.
∎

Remark 4.4.

We define the L. U. B. and G. L. B. of a sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
as a specialized
\mathrm{PQPT}
(i.e. the limit of a restricted sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s),
rather than directly introducing their general form into the syntax of a
\mathrm{PQPT}
,
due to the fact that the current one is enough for the follow-up development
while keeping a simple form of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s.

4.3.
Program correctness and expressiveness

\mathit{wp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).P=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n)}.P

\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).P=\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wlp}.S_{i}^{(n)}.P

\mathit{wp}.\mathbf{bot}.P=0

\mathit{wlp}.\mathbf{bot}.P=I

\mathit{xp}.\mathbf{skip}.P=P

\mathit{xp}.(q:=|0\rangle).P=\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|P|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|

\mathit{xp}.(\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U).P=U^{\dagger}PU

\mathit{xp}.(S_{1};S_{2}).P=\mathit{xp}.S_{1}.(\mathit{xp}.S_{2}.P)

\mathit{xp}.\mathbf{if}.P=\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}(\mathit{xp}.S_{m}.P)M_{m}

Table 3
.
Definition of formal weakest (liberal) preconditions —
\mathit{xp}\in\{\mathit{wp},\mathit{wlp}\}
.

For now, a (legitimate) quantum partial (resp. total) correctness formula
can be defined as a quantum Hoare’s triple
\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}
(resp.
\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle
),
where
S
is a quantum program, and
P,Q
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q)
.
Since quantum programs can be viewed semantically as a
\mathrm{QOP}
,
to define the semantics of a quantum Hoare’s triple,
we first need to define the correctness semantics of a
\mathrm{QOP}
.

Definition 4.0 (Correctness of
\mathrm{QOP}
s).

Let
M
,
N
be
\mathrm{QPRD}
s and
\mathcal{E}
a
\mathrm{QOP}
.
We say that

(Partial correctness).:

\mathcal{E}
is partially correct w.r.t. precondition
M
and postcondition
N
, written
\{M\}\,\mathcal{E}\,\{N\}
, if

(2)

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}(M\rho)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}\big(N\mathcal{E}(\rho)\big)+\big[\mathit{tr}(\rho)-\mathit{tr}\big(\mathcal{E}(\rho)\big)\big],\quad\forall\rho.

(Total correctness).:

\mathcal{E}
is totally correct w.r.t. precondition
M
and postcondition
N
, written
\langle M\rangle\,\mathcal{E}\,\langle N\rangle
, if

(3)

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}(M\rho)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}\big(N\mathcal{E}(\rho)\big),\quad\forall\rho.

Remark 4.5.

Eq. (
2
) can be seen as a probabilistic version of the following statement:
if state
\rho
satisfies predicate
M
, then, applying operation
\mathcal{E}
to
\rho
,
either
\mathcal{E}
fails to terminate or the resulting state
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
satisfies predicate
N
;
and total correctness is a stronger version of partial correctness
by guaranteeing termination once the precondition is satisfied.
For more information on classical partial and total correctness,
the reader is referred to
(
Francez 1992
)
.

As in classical Hoare logic,
the notion of weakest (liberal) precondition can be a candidate for the definition of
intermediate assertions involved in proving quantum program correctness.
The semantical weakest (liberal) preconditions (for a
\mathrm{QOP}
) can be defined as:

Theorem 4.14

(Quantum duality theorem)
.

Let
N
be a
\mathrm{QPRD}
and
\mathcal{E}
a
\mathrm{QOP}
.
Define the semantical weakest (resp., liberal) precondition
\mathit{WP}(\mathcal{E},N)

(resp.,
\mathit{WLP}(\mathcal{E},N)
) of
\mathcal{E}
w.r.t.
N
by

•

\mathit{WP}(\mathcal{E},N)\triangleq\mathcal{E}^{*}(N)

•

\mathit{WLP}(\mathcal{E},N)\triangleq I-\mathit{WP}(\mathcal{E},I-N)

It is the case, for any
\mathrm{QPRD}

M
, that

(a):

\langle M\rangle\,\mathcal{E}\,\langle N\rangle
if, and only if,
M\sqsubseteq\mathit{WP}(\mathcal{E},N)
; and

(b):

\{M\}\,\mathcal{E}\,\{N\}
if, and only if,
M\sqsubseteq\mathit{WLP}(\mathcal{E},N)
.

Proof.

By Lem.
2.3
, together with definition of Schrödinger-Heisenberg dual.
∎

Quantum duality theorem implies that:
(a)
\mathit{WP}
of a
\mathrm{QOP}
can be represented by its Schrödinger-Heisenberg dual;
(b)
\mathit{WP}
and
\mathit{WLP}
are logically dual to each other.

Theorem 4.15

(Quantum expressiveness theorem)
.

Let quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
, and
P
a
\mathrm{PQPT}
.
Define the formal weakest (resp. liberal) precondition

\mathit{wp}.S.P
(resp.,
\mathit{wlp}.S.P
) of
S
w.r.t.
P
in Tab.
3
.
It is the case that

(a):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wp}.S.P=\llbracket S\rrbracket^{*}(P)
;

(b):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wlp}.S.P=I-\mathit{wp}.S.(I-P)

\big(\triangleq\mathit{wp}.S.P+(I-\mathit{wp}.S.I)\big)
.

Proof.

See App.
B.2
.
∎

Lemma 4.0

(Well-definedness of
\mathit{wp}
and
\mathit{wlp}
)
.

Let quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
, and
P
a
\mathrm{PQPT}
.
It is the case that

(a):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n)}.P\sqsubseteq\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n+1)}.P
, for all
n\geq 0
;

(b):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wlp}.S_{i}^{(n)}.P\sqsupseteq\mathit{wlp}.S_{i}^{(n+1)}.P
, for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

By Lems.
3.5
,
4.12
, and Thm.
4.15
.
∎

Definition 4.0 (Correctness of quantum programs).

Let
P
,
Q
be
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q)
, and
S
a quantum program.
We say that

(Partial correctness).:

S
is partially correct w.r.t. precondition
P
and postcondition
Q
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
, denoted
\models_{\mathbb{I}}\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}
, if
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq\mathit{wlp}.S.Q
;

(Total correctness).:

S
is totally correct w.r.t. precondition
P
and postcondition
Q
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
, denoted
\models_{\mathbb{I}}\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle
, if
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq\mathit{wp}.S.Q
.

Remark 4.6.

Quantum duality and expressiveness theorems together entail that

\mathit{wp}.S.Q
(resp.
\mathit{wlp}.S.Q
) is the weakest
\mathrm{PQPT}

R
s.t.

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\langle R\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle
(resp.
\models_{\mathbb{I}}\{R\}\,S\,\{Q\}
).
This justifies the well-definedness of correctness of quantum programs (cf. Def.
4.17
),
which can be seen as a parameterized extension of correctness of
\mathrm{QOP}
s (cf. Def.
4.13
).

5.
Starting proof systems

(A Bot)

\{I\}\,\mathbf{bot}\,\{P\}

(
resp.
\langle 0\rangle\,\mathbf{bot}\,\langle P\rangle
)

(A Skip)

\{P\}\,\mathbf{skip}\,\{P\}

(A Init)

\dfrac{\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|=I_{q}}{\{\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|P|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|\}\,q:=|0\rangle\,\{P\}}

(A Unit)

\dfrac{UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=I_{\bar{q}}}{\{U^{\dagger}PU\}\,\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U\,\{P\}}

(R Comp)

\dfrac{\{P\}\,S_{1}\,\{Q\}\quad\{Q\}\,S_{2}\,\{R\}}{\{P\}\,S_{1};S_{2}\,\{R\}}

(R Case)

\dfrac{\{P_{m}\}\,S_{m}\,\{Q\}\mbox{ \small for each }m}{\{\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}P_{m}M_{m}\}\,\mathbf{if}\,\{Q\}}

(R Order)

\dfrac{P\sqsubseteq P^{\prime}\quad\{P^{\prime}\}\,S\,\{Q^{\prime}\}\quad Q^{\prime}\sqsubseteq Q}{\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}}

(R Subst)

\dfrac{\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}}{\{P[R/\mathcal{X}]\}\,S\,\{Q[R/\mathcal{X}]\}}

Table 4
.
Base proof system
\mathit{qBS}
.

This section is devoted to presenting different axiom systems
for proving partial, total and even probabilistic correctness of recursive quantum programs
\mathit{RqPL}
.

Base proof system.

The first step is to present an extension
\mathit{qBS}
(quantum Base System) to part of proof system
\mathit{qPD}
of
(
Ying 2011
)

for both partial and total correctness of quantum base language
\mathit{qPL}
,
so that we can deal with syntactic pre- and post-conditions (i.e.
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).
Every formula of
\mathit{qBS}
is either a legitimate quantum Hoare’s triple
\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}
