or
\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle

(
where
P,Q
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q)
)
,
or a legitimate Löwner ordering formula
P\sqsubseteq Q
or
P=Q

(
where
P,Q
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\mathit{Prmt}(P)=\mathit{Prmt}(Q)
)
.

Proof system
\mathit{qBS}
features the newly added inference rule — (R Subst) — handling the substitution in
\mathrm{PQPT}
s,
where
R
(resp.
\mathcal{X}
) is an arbitrary
\mathrm{PQPT}
(resp. quantum predicate variable),
and
P[R/\mathcal{X}]
stands for the result of simultaneously substituting
R
for each occurrence of
\mathcal{X}
in
P
.
For the presentation of
\mathit{qBS}
, the reader is referred to Tab.
4
.

Remark 5.1.

Every proof rule of
\mathit{qBS}
except for (A Bot) is only presented in the form of partial correctness formulas,
but nevertheless, applies to proving total correctness.
To see this, we note that partial and total correctness are distinguished by whether terminating almost surely
(cf. Rem.
4.5
).
This justifies why
\mathbf{bot}
and recursive procedures need a distinguish between partial and total correctness proof rules,
because they are sources of non-termination.

Intuition behind
\mathit{qBS}
.

To see the intuition of proof rules in
\mathit{qBS}
, we remark that

•

(A Bot, A Skip, A Init, A Unit) have the form
\{\mathit{xp}.S.P\}\,S\,\{P\}
;

•

(R Comp, R Case, R Subst) preserve the form
\{\mathit{xp}.S.P\}\,S\,\{P\}
(bidirectionally);

•

(R Order) can be used to relax
\{\mathit{xp}.S.P\}\,S\,\{P\}
to
\{Q\}\,S\,\{P\}
with

\models_{\mathbb{I}}Q\sqsubseteq\mathit{xp}.S.P
.

For the proof rule (R Case), the annotated
\mathbf{if}
-statement is illustrated as follows.

\{l_{1}:P\}\,\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow\{l_{2}^{m}:P_{m}\}\,S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}\,\{l_{3}:Q\}

Fix the input
\rho
(at program point
l_{1}
).
By semantics of the
\mathbf{if}
-statement,
every post-measurement state
M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{{\dagger}}
(containing the probability of observing outcome
m
)
will go to the corresponding branch labeled by
l_{2}^{m}
in which
P_{m}
should be satisfied and
S_{m}
will be executed.
By the Turing-Floyd-Hoare principle, we have that

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}(P\rho)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathit{tr}(P_{m}M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{{\dagger}})

Due to the arbitrariness of
\rho
, by properties of
\mathit{tr}
and
\sqsubseteq
,
it follows that
P\sqsubseteq\sum_{m}M_{m}^{{\dagger}}P_{m}M_{m}
.
Note that after the execution of each
S_{m}
,
the program point
l_{3}
is reached and the attached assertion
Q
is satisfied.
By weakening
P
to
\sum_{m}M_{m}^{{\dagger}}P_{m}M_{m}
and lifting the above reasoning process into a proof rule,
the inference rule (R Case) follows.
Weakening
P
to
\sum_{m}M_{m}^{{\dagger}}P_{m}M_{m}
guarantees that
(R Case) preserves the form
\{\mathit{xp}.S.P\}\,S\,\{P\}
forward (i.e. compact soundness).
To make (R Case) preserve the form
\{\mathit{xp}.S.P\}\,S\,\{P\}
backward (i.e. compact completeness),
we have to choose
P_{m}
as
\mathit{xp}.S_{m}.Q
for each
m
.

Soundness and Completeness.

Of not only theoretical but also practical interest is
the question of soundness and completeness of proof systems presented as before or after.
The question of soundness concerns the correctness of the method,
whereas the question of completeness concerns the scope of its applicability
(under what circumstances it can be successfully applied).
(For a systematic introduction to the soundness and completeness issues of classical Hoare logic,
the reader is referred to the famous survey paper
(
Apt 1981
)
.)

For presentational convenience in what follows,
assume that all formulas
F
are legitimate quantum Hoare’s triples or Löwner ordering formulas.
For sets of formulas
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle B

means that if
\models_{\mathbb{I}}A
then
\models_{\mathbb{I}}B
,
where by
\models_{\mathbb{I}}A
is meant that for all formulas
F
of
A
,
\models_{\mathbb{I}}F
.
Let
T
be a quantum assertion theory (e.g.
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}
).
For a proof system
\mathbf{H}
, e.g.
\mathit{qPD}
, by

\displaystyle T,A

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{F\in B}F

is meant that
every
formula of
B
can be deduced from
T
,
A
,
or axioms of
\mathbf{H}
by finitely applying inference rules of
\mathbf{H}
.
(We can replace
\vdash_{\mathbf{H}}
by
\vdash
, if
\mathbf{H}
is clear from the context.)
Note that the assertion theory
T
is used to provide Löwner ordering formulas
as antecedents of the inference rule (R Order).
Let
\mathbf{H}
be for the programming language
L
. We say that

•

\mathbf{H}
is sound, if for all Hoare’s triples
F
of
L
with
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}\vdash_{\mathbf{H}}F
,
we have
\models_{\mathbb{I}}F
;

•

\mathbf{H}
is (relatively) complete, if for all Hoare’s triples
F
of
L
with
\models_{\mathbb{I}}F
,
we have
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}\vdash_{\mathbf{H}}F
.

Note that
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}
provides all true Löwner ordering formulas for (R Order),
which, together with the condition of expressiveness (cf. Subsec.
4.3
),
is sufficient to make
\mathbf{H}
complete
(
Cook 1978
;
Bergstra and
Tucker 1982
)

(
\mathrm{QPRD}
s are enough for while loops
(
Ying 2011
)
;
while general recursive procedures need
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).
As with
\mathit{qPD}
, the proof system
\mathit{qBS}
(for the base language
\mathit{qPL}
) is sound and complete.

Not to mention it explicitly,
various proof systems presented in the sequel are sound and complete
in the above sense (e.g. Lem.
5.3
),
except that a compact version of soundness and completeness
is introduced for exact probabilistic reasoning (cf. Lem.
5.5
).
For a complete proof of these soundness and completeness results,
the reader is referred to App.
D
.

Remark 5.2.

The above discussion on soundness and completeness issues is purely theoretical,
because we adopt the assertion theory as an oracle
(rather than as a recursively axiomatizable theory),
following the technical line of classical Hoare logic
(
Cook 1978
)
.
In practice, as discussed in Rem.
4.3
,
a restricted Löwner comparison between
\mathrm{PQPT}
s independent of parameters
is enough to cover the correctness checking of Löwner ordering formulas.

5.1.
Partial correctness

\dfrac{\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q\}\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}}{\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q\}}

(a)

(
\rm{Rp}
Rec).

\dfrac{\big\{\{P_{i}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\{Q_{i}\}\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\{P_{i}\}\,S_{i}\,\{Q_{i}\}}{\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\{P_{i}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\{Q_{i}\}}

(b)

(
\rm{Rp}
gRec).

Table 5
.
Proof rules for partial correctness.

We are now in a position to present inference rules for proving partial correctness of recursive procedures.
We begin with the case of simple recursion.

Simple recursion.

Consider first the case of simple recursion, that is that,
the body
S
of recursive quantum procedure
\mathit{proc}
should itself contain the re-invocation statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
,
but retain the exclusion of invoking other recursive quantum procedures.
The proof rule — (
\rm{Rp}
Rec) — for proving partial correctness of
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
is shown in Tab.
5
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\{l_{0}:P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{l_{1}:Q\}\end{array}

(a)

Annotated program for
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
.

\{l_{2}:P\}\cdots\{l_{3}:P^{\prime}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{l_{4}:Q^{\prime}\}\cdots\{l_{5}:P^{\prime\prime}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{l_{6}:Q^{\prime\prime}\}\cdots\{l_{7}:Q\}

(b)

Annotated program for the body
S
.

Table 6
.
Intermediate assertion method — labels (
l_{0}
-
l_{7}
) are used to indicate different program points;
and each program point is annotated with a
\mathrm{PQPT}
.

Intuition of (Rp Rec).

To derive the correctness formula
\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q\}
about
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
,
it suffices to derive
\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}
for its body
S
(cf. Tab.
6
);
since
S
itself contains the re-invocation statement (or inner)
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
,
it suffices to derive correctness formulas about the inner
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
, say
\{P^{\prime}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q^{\prime}\}
;
by the Turing-Floyd-Hoare Principle,
\{P^{\prime}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q^{\prime}\}
should be adapted
from the premise
\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q\}
possibly by using (R Subst).

(
In this case, data flow goes first from
l_{3}
to
l_{0}
; and then from
l_{1}
to
l_{4}
.
The case of
\{P^{\prime\prime}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q^{\prime\prime}\}
can be analyzed similarly.
)

This reveals the reason for introducing (R Subst):
without it the above derivation might not proceed as desired.

Example 5.0 (Counterexample, cf. App.
C.1
).

Let
q
be a quantum variable with
\it type(q)=\mathbf{Int}
.
We define the
(+i)
-operator
U_{+i}
over the computational basis of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
by

U_{+i}\colon|x\rangle\rightarrow|x+i\rangle

and similarly for the
(-i)
-operator
U_{-i}
.
Declare the procedure
\mathit{t}oy
by

\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{t}oy\rangle\colon\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}

with
M\triangleq\big\{M_{0}=\sum_{i\leq 0}|i\rangle\langle i|,\ M_{1}=\sum_{i\geq 1}|i\rangle\langle i|\big\}

and
\{S_{m}\}_{m=0,1}
defined by

S_{0}\triangleq\mathbf{skip},\quad S_{1}\triangleq q\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{-1};\ \mathbf{call}\ \mathit{t}oy;\ q\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{+1}

Fix
n\geq 0
. We can derive the partial correctness formula

\{|n\rangle_{q}\langle n|\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{t}oy\,\{|n\rangle_{q}\langle n|\}

by using (
\rm{Rp}
Rec). However, this is not the case if the use of (R Subst) is disallowed.

General recursion.

We now extend (Rp Rec) for simple recursion to the general case.
For (mutual) recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
with body
S_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
the inference rule (Rp gRec) is introduced to prove their partial correctness in a simultaneous way
(cf. Tab.
5
).

Remark 5.3.

Suppose that the procedure
\mathit{proc}
with body
S
has no re-invocation,
then the inference rule (Rp Rec) will be degenerated to

\mbox{(R Proc)}\quad\dfrac{\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}}{\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\{Q\}}

which is precisely the inference rule for non-recursive procedures.
In other words,
the proof rule (R Proc) for non-recursive procedures is a special case of (Rp Rec) for recursive procedures.
Also, the proof rule (Rp Rec) for simple recursion can be seen as a special case of (Rp gRec) for general recursion,
if the index variable
i
is required to range over a singleton.

Synthesis of recursive invariants.

When proof rule (Rp gRec) is successfully applied to
proving partial correctness formula
\{P_{i}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\{Q_{i}\}
for recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq n
, we call
(P_{i},Q_{i})
a recursive invariant of
\mathit{proc}_{i}
,
where
\mathrm{PQPT}

P_{i}
can be replaced by
\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).Q_{i}
(cf. Tab.
3
),
which has the following form

\displaystyle\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).Q_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\rrbracket^{*}(Q_{i})+\big(I-\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\rrbracket^{*}(I)\big)

where
\big\{\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\rrbracket\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is the least fixed point of
\mathcal{F}

(cf. Rem.
3.3
and Thm.
4.15
).
However,
\mathrm{PQPT}

Q_{i}
sometimes should be parameterized,
and the substitution for parameters will highly depend on
\mathit{proc}_{i}
itself (cf. Exm.
5.1
),
which means that there is no uniform characterization, say fixed-point characterization,
for the recursive invariant
\big(\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).Q_{i},Q_{i}\big)
.
In other words, the synthesis of recursive invariants is generally not purely automatic,
yet
\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).Q_{i}
can be automatically synthesised provided
Q_{i}
is given.

The scope of applicability.

Recalling the semantical base of a partial-correctness formula,
i.e. Eq. (
2
) in Def.
4.13
,
one can see that it is a straightforward extension of classical partial-correctness semantics for deterministic programs.
Thus, (Rp gRec) is applicable to reasoning about programs with “deterministic control and quantum data”.
For this purpose, we typically use quantum variables to model classical variables,
i.e. encode classical values as states of a computational basis.
See, e.g., case studies.

On the other hand, our programming language should include
nondeterministic quantum programs (i.e. those branched by non-deterministic quantum observations),
where each nondeterministic branch is associated with an exact probability
(encoded into states).
For these quantum programs (with “probabilistic control and quantum data”),
we need to do reasoning with exact probability,
say, given a precondition, with what probability a program
will output a particular state (or a particular class of states) or terminate?
E.g., quantum program
\mathrm{RQMC}
on any input always outputs
|+\rangle
with probability
\frac{1}{3}

(cf. Exm.
3.6
), i.e.

(4)

\displaystyle\forall\rho.\ \mathit{tr}(I\rho)=1

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathit{tr}\big(|+\rangle\langle+|\llbracket\mathrm{RQMC}\rrbracket(\rho)\big)=\frac{1}{3}

Unfortunately, interfered by probability of nontermination, i.e.
\mathit{tr}(\rho)-\mathit{tr}\big(\llbracket\mathrm{RQMC}\rrbracket(\rho)\big)
,
partial-correctness semantics fails to fully express Ass. (
4
).
Therefore, (Rp gRec) is not very suitable for
reasoning about programs with “probabilistic control and quantum data”.

5.2.
Total correctness

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{c}\exists\ \{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}\mbox{ with }P_{0}=0\mbox{ s.t.}\\
\langle P_{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\langle Q\rangle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}\langle P_{n+1}\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ for all }n\geq 0,\\
P\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}\end{array}}{\langle P\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}\,\langle Q\rangle}

(a)

(
\rm{Rt}
Rec).

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq n,\ \exists\ \{P_{i}^{j}\}_{j\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}\mbox{ with }P_{i}^{0}=0\mbox{ s.t.}\\
\big\{\langle P_{i}^{j}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\langle Q_{i}\rangle\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\langle P_{i}^{j+1}\rangle\,S_{i}\,\langle Q_{i}\rangle\mbox{ for all }j\geq 0,\\
P_{i}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{\infty}P_{i}^{j}\end{array}}{\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\langle P_{i}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\langle Q_{i}\rangle}

(b)

(
\rm{Rt}
gRec).

Table 7
.
Proof rules for total correctness.

We are now positioned to present inference rules for proving total correctness of recursive procedures.
To begin with, recall from Lem.
4.12
that

\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
is an increasing sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s (ordered by
\sqsubseteq
)
defined by
\big\{P_{n}\triangleq\mathcal{E}_{n}^{*}(R)\big\}_{n\geq 0}
,
where
R
is a
\mathrm{PQPT}
and
\{\mathcal{E}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is an increasing sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s (also ordered by
\sqsubseteq
).

Simple recursion.

To deal with the termination problem of recursive procedure
\mathit{proc}
(cf. Tab.
6
),
introduce a sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
with
P_{0}=0
.
Intuitively, if the entry point of
\mathit{proc}
(say
l_{0}
) is attached currently with assertion
P_{n+1}
,
then, upon re-invocation in the body
S
of
\mathit{proc}
,
the data flow at entry points of inner
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
(e.g.,
l_{3}
or
l_{5}
)
needs to be constrained by a stronger assertion,
namely
P_{n}
, or its substitution by using (R Subst).
Finally, to cease re-invocation, i.e. treating inner
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
as
\mathbf{bot}
,
the attached assertion at entry points should be
0
, namely
P_{0}
.
Combining this idea with (Rp Rec),
we thus obtain the inference rule (Rt Rec) for proving total correctness of
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}
(cf. Tab.
7
).

Example 5.0 (Counterexample, cf. App.
C.2
).

Let the recursive procedure
\mathit{t}oy
be as defined in Exm.
5.1
.
Fix
n\geq 0
. We can derive the total correctness formula

\langle|n\rangle_{q}\langle n|\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{t}oy\,\langle|n\rangle_{q}\langle n|\rangle

by using (Rt Rec). However, this is not the case if the use of (R Subst) is disallowed.

General recursion.

We now extend the (Rt Rec) for simple recursion to the general case.
For recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
with body
S_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
the inference rule (Rt gRec) is introduced to simultaneously prove their total correctness (cf. Tab.
7
).
Note that (Rt Rec) can be seen as a special case of (Rt gRec), if the index
i
is required to range over a singleton.

Remark 5.4.

In applications, sequences of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\{P_{i}^{j}\}^{\sqsubseteq}_{j\geq 0}
with
P_{i}^{0}=0
in (Rt gRec)

(
or, equivalently, sequences of
\mathrm{QOP}
s
\{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{j}\}^{\sqsubseteq}_{j\geq 0}
with
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{0}=0\diamond 0
,
cf. Lem.
4.12
)

usually have a closed form with
j
as an (index) variable or are defined by induction on
j
,
thus the statement

\displaystyle\big\{\big\langle P_{i}^{j}\big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}\,\big\langle Q_{i}\big\rangle\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\big\langle P_{i}^{j+1}\big\rangle\,S_{i}\,\big\langle Q_{i}\big\rangle,\quad\mbox{ for all }j\geq 0

can be proved either for one pass by treating
j
as an arbitrary (but fixed) variable,
or for two passes by induction on
j
(one for the basis and the other for the inductive step).

Synthesis of intermediate assertions.

To make (Rt gRec) be successfully applied to
proving total correctness of recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
we need to provide the intermediate assertions
\{P_{i}^{j}\}_{j\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
and
Q_{i}
involved.
To this end,
P_{i}^{j}
can be replaced by
\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(j)}.Q_{i}

(
in this case
P_{i}
can be selected as
\mathit{wp}.(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}).Q_{i}
)
,
which has the following form

\displaystyle\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(j)}.Q_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket S_{i}^{(j)}\rrbracket^{*}(Q_{i})

where
\big\{\llbracket S_{i}^{(j)}\rrbracket\big\}_{j\geq 0}
with
1\leq i\leq n

is the least sequence of
\mathrm{QOP}
s generated by
\mathcal{F}
(cf. Rem.
3.3
and Thm.
4.15
).
As in the case of recursive invariants, there is no uniform (fixed-point) characterization for the
\mathrm{PQPT}
s

\big\{\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(j)}.Q_{i}\big\}_{j\geq 0}
and
Q_{i}
(entailed by Exm.
5.2
).
Therefore, the synthesis of these intermediate assertions is semi-automatic, that is to say that,
the assertions
\big\{\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(j)}.Q_{i}\big\}_{j\geq 0}
can be automatically synthesised provided that
Q_{i}
is given.

The scope of applicability.

Recalling Ass. (
3
) in Def.
4.13
,
one can see that the total-correctness semantics for quantum programs
is a natural extension of classical counterpart for deterministic programs.
Thus, (Rt gRec) is applicable to reasoning about programs with “deterministic control and quantum data”.
See, for example, case studies.

However, due to inequality in Ass. (
3
), this (general) version of total-correctness semantics
can merely be used for reasoning with approximate probabilities, and thus fails to support precise probabilistic reasoning,
e.g. precisely describing Ass. (
4
), as in the case of partial correctness.
Fortunately, a restrictive use of (Rt gRec) applies to
reasoning with exact probabilities about programs with “probabilistic control and quantum data”.
We shall develop an axiomatic basis for (approximate or exact) probabilistic reasoning as follows.

5.3.
Probabilistic correctness

Reasoning with approximate probabilities

As discussed above,
Ass. (
3
) can be used for the semantical basis of reasoning with approximate probabilities.
Then an axiomatic basis of the (approximate) probabilistic correctness follows from the soundness and completeness lemma.

Lemma 5.0

(Soundness and completeness)
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
and any
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
P,Q
, it is the case that

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}\vdash\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ if and only if }\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubseteq\mathit{wp}.S.Q

Proof.

By Def.
4.17
and Thm.
D.5
.
∎

Theorem 5.4

(Reasoning with approximate probabilities)
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
, any
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
P,Q
and any
\delta\in[0,1]
, it is the case that

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}}\vdash\langle\delta P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ if and only if }\forall\rho.\ \mathit{tr}(P\rho)=1\implies\mathit{tr}\big(Q\llbracket S\rrbracket(\rho)\big)\geq\delta

Proof.

Contained in the proof of Thm.
D.6
.
∎

Reasoning with exact probabilities

A semantical basis of (exact) probabilistic reasoning
can be adapted from Eq. (
3
) with
=
in place of
\leq
(for approximate reasoning).
Based on this, the semantics of a total-correctness formula

\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle
has the following property

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ if and only if }\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=\mathit{wp}.S.Q

To build an axiomatic basis of this (exact) probabilistic correctness,
we propose the concept of compact soundness and completeness in Lem.
5.5
, and, as a consequence,
a (syntactically checkable) condition for exact probabilistic reasoning is identified in Thm.
5.6
.

Lemma 5.0

(Compact soundness and completeness)
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
and any
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
P,Q
, it is the case that

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}\vdash\langle P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ if and only if }\models_{\mathbb{I}}P=\mathit{wp}.S.Q

Proof.

Contained in the proof of Thm.
D.5
.
∎

Theorem 5.6

(Reasoning with exact probabilities)
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{RqPL}
, any
\mathrm{QPRD}
s
P,Q
and any
\delta\in[0,1]
, it is the case that

{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}\vdash\langle\delta P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle\mbox{ if and only if }\forall\rho.\ \mathit{tr}(P\rho)=1\implies\mathit{tr}\big(Q\llbracket S\rrbracket(\rho)\big)=\delta

Proof.

Contained in the proof of Thm.
D.6
.
∎

Remark 5.5.

Thm.
5.6
(resp. Thm.
5.4
)
establishes an axiomatic basis for reasoning with exact (resp. approximate) probabilities.
Concretely speaking, if
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
P
and
Q
are chosen as projection operators,
then Hoare’s triple
\langle\delta P\rangle\,S\,\langle Q\rangle
is able to express that
“In case the inputs of
S
fall into the subspace
P
,
the outputs will fall into
Q
with probability
=\delta
(resp.
\leq\delta
)”.
In particular, when
P,Q
are the identity operator
I
,
Hoare’s triple
\langle\delta I\rangle\,S\,\langle I\rangle
represents termination on any input with probability
=\delta
(resp.
\leq\delta
); and
\langle I\rangle\,S\,\langle I\rangle
almost-sure termination in both cases.
Note that during the reasoning with exact probabilities,
the necessary Löwner ordering formulas are of the form
P=P^{\prime}
, provided by
{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}
.

Example 5.0 (Reasoning about
\mathrm{RQMC}
with exact probabilities).

Recall the game
\mathrm{RQMC}
from Exms.
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.6
.
We illustrate how to do reasoning with exact probabilities
by showing probabilistic correctness and probabilistic termination of
\mathrm{RQMC}
.

(i) (Probabilistic correctness).

To formally prove that Alice wins with probability
\frac{1}{3}
,
it suffices to prove the total correctness formula

\Big\langle\frac{1}{3}I\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{RQMC}\,\Big\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\Big\rangle

By (A Init, R Comp), it suffices to prove

\Big\langle\frac{|0\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|}{3}\Big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\,\Big\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\Big\rangle\mbox{ and }\Big\langle\frac{|0\rangle\langle 0|+4|1\rangle\langle 1|}{6}\Big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}\,\Big\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\Big\rangle

simultaneously. Defining
P_{A}^{n}
,
P_{B}^{n}
by

P_{A}^{n}\triangleq\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k-1\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)|0\rangle\langle 0|+\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)|1\rangle\langle 1|,\quad P_{B}^{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}P_{A}^{n-1}+\frac{1}{2}|1\rangle\langle 1|

and
\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n}
,
\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}
by

\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n}\triangleq\langle P_{A}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle,\quad\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}\triangleq\langle P_{B}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle

by (
\rm{Rt}
gRec), it suffices to prove, for all
n\geq 0
, that

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n},\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\langle P_{A}^{n+1}\rangle\,\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n},\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\langle P_{B}^{n+1}\rangle\,\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M^{\prime}[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}^{\prime}\ \mathbf{fi}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle

The proof is done by applying (R Case) to Hoare’s triples (1-3) and (4,5) respectively.

\begin{array}[]{clr}(1)&\langle|0\rangle\langle 0|\rangle\,q\;{\ast}{=}\;H\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle&\mbox{(A Unit)}\\
(2)&\langle P_{B}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle&\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}\\
(3)&\langle 0\rangle\,\mathbf{bot}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle&\mbox{(A Bot)}\\
(4)&\langle P_{A}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle&\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n}\\
(5)&\langle|1\rangle\langle 1|\rangle\,q\;{\ast}{=}\;HX\,\langle|+\rangle\langle+|\rangle&\mbox{(A Unit)}\end{array}

(ii) (Probabilistic termination).

To formally prove that
\mathrm{RQMC}
terminates with probability
\frac{2}{3}
,
it suffices to prove the total correctness formula

\Big\langle\frac{2}{3}I\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{RQMC}\,\Big\langle I\Big\rangle

By (A Init, R Comp), it suffices to prove

\Big\langle\frac{2}{3}I\Big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\,\Big\langle I\Big\rangle\mbox{ and }\Big\langle\frac{5}{6}I\Big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}\,\Big\langle I\Big\rangle

simultaneously. The proof proceeds as above, by redefining
P_{A}^{n}
,
P_{B}^{n}
by

P_{A}^{n}\triangleq\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k-1\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)I+\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}^{2k\leq n}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\Big)I,\quad P_{B}^{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{2}P_{A}^{n-1}+\frac{1}{2}I

and
\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n}
,
\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}
by

\mathit{Prem}_{A}^{n}\triangleq\langle P_{A}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Alice}\,\langle I\rangle,\quad\mathit{Prem}_{B}^{n}\triangleq\langle P_{B}^{n}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{Bob}\,\langle I\rangle

Remark 5.6 (Counterexample, cf. Thm.
D.3
).

The proof system for partial correctness of
\mathit{RqPL}
has no compact soundness.
To see this, suppose that
S\triangleq\mathbf{call}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}
(cf. Exam.
3.1
),
and
P,Q
are
\mathrm{PQPT}
s with
\models_{\mathbb{I}}P\sqsubset I
.
Then, by definition of
\mathit{wlp}
(cf. Tab.
3
), we have that

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle P\sqsubset I=\mathit{wlp}.(\mathbf{call}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}).Q

However, by (Rp pRec), it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{=}}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\{P\}\,\mathbf{call}\ P_{\mathbf{bot}}\,\{Q\}

This reveals that the standard intermediate assertion method for partial correctness
(i.e. the Turing-Floyd-Hoare Principle, cf. Tab.
6
)
can’t be used universally for reasoning about recursive procedures with exact probabilities
(even if involving nontermination).

5.4.
Proof rules for while loops

\dfrac{\{P\}\,S\,\{M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}\}}{\{M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}\}\,\mathbf{while}\ M[\bar{q}]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ S\ \mathbf{od}\,\{Q\}}

(a)

(
\rm{Rp}
Loop).

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{c}\exists\ \{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}\mbox{ with }P_{0}=0\mbox{ s.t.}\\
\langle P_{n+1}\rangle\,S\,\langle M_{0}^{\dagger}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{\dagger}P_{n}M_{1}\rangle\mbox{ for all }n\geq 0,\\
P\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}\end{array}}{\langle M_{0}^{\dagger}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{\dagger}PM_{1}\rangle\,\mathbf{while}\ M[\bar{q}]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ S\ \mathbf{od}\,\langle Q\rangle}

(b)

(
\rm{Rt}
Loop).

Table 8
.
Proof rules for while loops.

The while-loop program
\mathbf{while}\triangleq\mathbf{while}\ M[\bar{q}]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ S\ \mathbf{od}
with
M\triangleq\{M_{0},M_{1}\}
,
can be defined as a call of tail recursion
\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}
, where
T_{\mathbf{while}}
has the body

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi},

with
S_{0}\triangleq\mathbf{skip}
and
S_{1}\triangleq S;\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}
.

Partial correctness.

To derive
\{R\}\,\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}\,\{Q\}
, by (
\rm{Rp}
Rec), it suffices to show

\displaystyle\{R\}\,\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}\,\{Q\}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\{R\}\,\mathbf{if}\,\{Q\}

By (R Case), together with (A Skip)
\{Q\}\,\mathbf{skip}\,\{Q\}
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\{R\}\,\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}\,\{Q\}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\{P\}\,S;\mathbf{call}\ T_{\mathbf{while}}\,\{Q\}

Here we let
R\triangleq M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}
.
By (R Comp), it suffices to derive

\{P\}\,S\,\{M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}\}

Thus, the proof rule (Rp Loop) for partial correctness of
\mathbf{while}
is designed in Tab.
8
.

Total correctness.

To prove total correctness of
\mathbf{while}
,
by (
\rm{Rt}
Rec),
we need to introduce a sequence of assertions at the same program point,
in which
R=M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}
lies,
each with a different time point.
Instead of doing so, introduce
\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
with
P_{0}=0

at the program point where
P
lies.
(We remark that each time the data flow enters the loop body, the assertion
P_{n}
will be encountered;
yet only after exiting the loop, should
Q
be met.)
Thus, the proof rule (
\rm{Rt}
Loop) for total correctness of
\mathbf{while}
can be designed in Tab.
8
.

Ying’s rules revisited.

Ying’s proof rule for partial correctness of while loops is the same as (
\rm{Rp}
Loop)
(
Ying 2011
)
.
However, his solution to solving the issue of termination is based on a (semantical) notion of
(P,\epsilon)
-boundedness,
where
\epsilon
bounds the trace of the diverging computation.
If, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there is a
(M_{1}^{\dagger}QM_{1},\epsilon)
-bound function of a while loop starting in
Q
,
then the loop terminates.
Thus, (
\rm{Rp}
Loop) is used there jointly with the above condition
to prove total correctness of while loops
(
Ying 2011
)
.

Remark 5.7.

Illustrated by the process of
deducing proof rules for while loops from those for recursive procedures (and also by Exm.
5.7
),
one can see that reasoning about a tail recursion doesn’t necessarily require (R Subst),
since the Hoare’s triple on a call statement as premise
can directly provide all the needed triples of that call statement in the body.

Lemma 5.0

(Cf. Props. 4.2.2 and 4.2.3 of
(
Ying 2016
)
)
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{while}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathbf{while}\ M[\bar{q}]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ S\ \mathbf{od}

with
M\triangleq\{M_{0},M_{1}\}
, and
Q
a (non-parameterized)
\mathrm{PQPT}
.
Define the
\mathrm{PQPT}

\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{xp}}^{Q}(\mathcal{X})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{xp}}^{Q}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}(\mathit{xp}.S.\mathcal{X})M_{1}

where
\mathit{xp}\in\{\mathit{wp},\mathit{wlp}\}
. It is the case that

(i):

\mathit{wlp}.\mathbf{while}.Q=\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{n}
,
where
Q_{0}\triangleq I
and
Q_{n+1}\triangleq\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{wlp}}^{Q}(Q_{n})
, for all
n\geq 0
;

(ii):

\mathit{wp}.\mathbf{while}.Q=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}
,
where
P_{0}\triangleq 0
and
P_{n+1}\triangleq\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{wp}}^{Q}(P_{n})
, for all
n\geq 0
.

Synthesis of intermediate assertions.

When applying (Rp Loop) to proving partial correctness of a while loop,
we have to provide the loop invariant
M_{0}^{{\dagger}}QM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}PM_{1}
,
which can be selected as
\mathit{wlp}.\mathbf{while}.Q
.
Note that
\mathit{wlp}.\mathbf{while}.Q
is the greatest fixed point of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{wlp}}^{Q}(\mathcal{X})
.
Similarly, in case of applying (Rt Loop),
we need to provide the intermediate assertions
\{P_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
which can be the lease sequence of assertions generated by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{wp}}^{Q}(\mathcal{X})
.

Example 5.0 (Almost-sure termination).

The following while loop

\mathbf{while}\ M[q]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ \mathbf{skip}\ \mathbf{od}

with
M\triangleq\{M_{0}=M_{1}\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}I_{q}\}

is abstracted from quantum random walks with absorbing boundaries (modeled by quantum measurements)
(
Bach et al. 2004
)

and quantum Bernoulli factory for random number generation
(
Dale
et al. 2015
)
.
To show its almost-sure termination, it suffices to prove the total correctness formula

\langle I\rangle\,\mathbf{while}\ M[q]=1\ \mathbf{do}\ \mathbf{skip}\ \mathbf{od}\,\langle I\rangle

by using (Rt Loop), where the assertions
\{P_{n}\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{i}}I_{q}\}_{n\geq 0}
is generated by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathit{wp}}^{I}(\mathcal{X})=M_{0}^{{\dagger}}IM_{0}+M_{1}^{{\dagger}}(\mathit{wp}.\mathbf{skip}.\mathcal{X})M_{1}=\frac{1}{2}I_{q}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{X}

Remark 5.8.

Observe that the necessary intermediate assertions
in proving correctness of while loops
have a uniform (fixed-point) characterization,
yet this is not always the case for (non-tail) recursion.
This observation, jointly with Rem.
5.7
, entails that
recursion is essentially more complex than while loops in the setting of program logics.

6.
Expanded proof systems

P

\triangleq

D;S

D

\triangleq

\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}\rangle(\bar{y})\colon S

S

\triangleq

\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\mid\mathbf{call}\ \langle\mathit{proc}\rangle(\bar{p})\mid\mathbf{bot}\mid\mathbf{skip}

\mid q:=|0\rangle\mid\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U\mid S_{1};S_{2}\mid\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}

(a)

Syntax of
\mathit{eRqPL}
.

(Loc)

\dfrac{\bar{r}\cap\mathit{Var}(\rho)=\emptyset,\ |\bar{r}|=|\bar{q}|,\ \it type(r_{i})=\it type(q_{i})\ \forall\ i}{\langle\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}];\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle}

(Rel)

\dfrac{tr_{\bar{r}}\triangleq\sum_{i}\langle i|\diamond|i\rangle\mbox{ with }\sum_{i}|i\rangle\langle i|=I_{\bar{r}}}{\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,tr_{\bar{r}}(\rho)\rangle}

(Proc)

\dfrac{\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{y})\colon S\in D}{\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{p}),\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle S[\bar{p}/\bar{y}],\rho\rangle}

(b)

Labeled transition rules for auxiliary facilities.

(Loc)

(Proc)

Denotational semantics for auxiliary facilities.

\mathit{wp}.\big(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\big).P

\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}].P

\mathit{wlp}.\big(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\big).P

\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wlp}.S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}].P

\mathit{xp}(\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}).P
