\langle 0|_{\bar{r}}\big(\mathit{xp}.S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}].(P\otimes I_{\bar{r}})\big)|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}

(d)

\mathit{wp}
and
\mathit{wlp}
for auxiliary facilities —
\mathit{xp}\in\{\mathit{wp},\mathit{wlp}\}
.

(R Loc)

\dfrac{\{P\otimes I_{\bar{r}}\}\,\bar{r}:=|0\rangle;S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\,\{Q\otimes I_{\bar{r}}\}}{\{P\}\,\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\,\{Q\}}

(R Adap)

\dfrac{\{P\}\,S\,\{Q\}}{\{P[\bar{p}/\bar{q}]\}\,S[\bar{p}/\bar{q}]\,\{Q[\bar{p}/\bar{q}]\}}

(Rp pRec)

\dfrac{\big\{\{P_{i}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})\,\{Q_{i}\}\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBE}}}\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\{P_{i}\}\,S_{i}\,\{Q_{i}\}}{\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\{P_{i}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})\,\{Q_{i}\}}

(Rt pRec)

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq n,\ \exists\ \{P_{i}^{j}\}_{j\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}\mbox{ with }P_{i}^{0}=0\mbox{ s.t.}\\
\big\{\big\langle P_{i}^{j}\big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})\,\big\langle Q_{i}\big\rangle\big\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBE}}}\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\big\langle P_{i}^{j+1}\big\rangle\,S_{i}\,\big\langle Q_{i}\big\rangle\mbox{ for all }j\geq 0,\\
P_{i}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{\infty}P_{i}^{j}\end{array}}{\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\langle P_{i}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})\,\langle Q_{i}\rangle}

(e)

Proof rules for auxiliary facilities.

Table 9
.
QHL for
\mathit{eRqPL}
—
\mathit{qBE}\triangleq\mathit{qBS}+\mbox{(R Loc)}+\mbox{(R Adap)}
.

In this section we augment the language
\mathit{RqPL}
with facilities of variable localization and parameter passing,
with which the applicability scope of recursive quantum programs will be broadened.
This argumentation is also in line with the spirit of
\mathrm{QPL}

(
Selinger 2004a
)
.

6.1.
Quantum variable localization

Definition of the syntax.

The construct of variable localization allows variables whose value is accessible only in a specified program fragment.
The syntax of such a construct with header
\mathbf{Loc}\ \langle\mathit{qvar\_list}\rangle
, body
S
and tailer
\mathbf{Rel}\ \langle\mathit{qvar\_list}\rangle
is given by

\mathbf{Loc}\ \langle\mathit{qvar\_list}\rangle;\ S;\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \langle\mathit{qvar\_list}\rangle

Example 6.0 (The system-environment model of a
\mathrm{QOP}
).

The dynamics of an open quantum system (modeled by quantum variables
\bar{q}
),
interacted by a unitary interaction
U
with an environment (modeled by
\bar{p}
with initial state
|0\rangle
),
can be programmed as a structure of quantum variable localization:

\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{p};\ (\bar{p},\bar{q})\;{\ast}{=}\;U;\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{p}

For instance, we can use this structure to program a circuit implementation
for the controlled operation
C^{n}(U)
in Fig. 4.10 of
(
Nielsen and
Chuang 2000
)
.
The circuit makes use of a small number
(n-1)
of working qubits, which all start and end in the state
|0\rangle
.

Definition of the semantics.

The intended meaning of the construct of variable localization is first expanding the state
with the default value
|0\rangle
of local variables
\mathit{qvar\_list}

declared by the header,
then executing the body possibly accessing
\mathit{qvar\_list}
,
and finally releasing
\mathit{qvar\_list}
by the tailer.
Since the names of local variables, say
\bar{q}
, may conflict with those of state variables outside the structure,
to define the formal semantics of variable localization, we need a reservoir of fresh quantum variables,
say
\bar{r}
(of the same length and of the same componentwise type as
\bar{q}
), to be used to express different instances of the local variables before binding them to values.
We can use partial trace function, say
tr_{\mathcal{H}_{\bar{r}}}
(abbr.
tr_{\bar{r}}
),
to define the formal semantics of quantum variable localization
(cf. Tabs.
9
and
9
).

Proof rules for the correctness.

We invent the proof rule — (R Loc)
— for proving both partial and total correctness of variable localization (cf. Tab.
9
).
Here, by convention,
P\otimes I_{\bar{r}}
and
Q\otimes I_{\bar{r}}
can be simplified to
P
and
Q
respectively.

Intuition of (R Loc).

Note that
\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}
is semantically equivalent to

\bar{r}:=|0\rangle;S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]
,
if the local variables
\bar{q}
are thought of as the fresh global variables
\bar{r}
.
Under this assumption, Hoare’s triple
\{P\}\,\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\,\{Q\}

is semantically equivalent to

(5)

\{P\otimes I_{\bar{r}}\}\,\bar{r}:=|0\rangle;S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\,\{Q\otimes I_{\bar{r}}\}

By lifting this semantical equivalence to the syntactical case, (R Loc) follows naturally.

If, on the other hand, we choose to substitute
\bar{r}
for
\bar{q}
in assertions instead of in programs,
then we find that Hoare’s triple
\{P\}\,\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\,\{Q\}

is semantically equivalent to

(6)

\{P[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{q}}\langle 0|\}\,S\,\{Q[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\otimes I_{\bar{q}}\}

By elevating this semantical deduction to an inference rule, we obtain

\mbox{(R' Loc)}\quad\dfrac{\{P[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{q}}\langle 0|\}\,S\,\{Q[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\otimes I_{\bar{q}}\}}{\{P\}\,\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\,\{Q\}}

Comparison of (R Loc) and (R’ Loc).

To show the (syntactic) equivalence of the two proof rules,
it suffices to show Hoare’s triples (
5
) and (
6
) can be transformed to each other.
This is the case by using (R Adap) (cf. Tab.
9
), (A Init) and (R Order).

To see the difference of the two proof rules,
we remark that (R Loc) is more in line with the formal semantics
and weakest (liberal) preconditions of variable localization (cf. Tabs.
9
and
9
),
but (R’ Loc) is purely inductive and thus more applicable in practice.

Example 6.0 (Grover’s search).

In Grover’s original search algorithm (cf. Chap. 6 of
(
Nielsen and
Chuang 2000
)
),
we can use a (unitary) oracle
O
, defined by its action on the computational basis:

\displaystyle|x\rangle|y\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{O}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle|x\rangle|y\oplus f(x)\rangle

to check whether an item
x
is a solution to the search problem.
Note that
f
is the characteristic function of the search problem,
and the oracle ancilla
|y\rangle
is a single qubit which is flipped if
f(x)=1
,
and is unchanged otherwise.
It is useful to initialize the oracle ancilla in state
|-\rangle
,
in which case the state of the ancilla is not changed,
and
f(x)
will occur as the exponent of a factor
(-1)^{f(x)}
of relative phases.
Thus the action of the oracle can be rewritten:

\displaystyle|\varphi\rangle\triangleq\sum_{x}\alpha_{x}|x\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{O}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle\triangleq\sum_{x}(-1)^{f(x)}\alpha_{x}|x\rangle

Let quantum variables
q
,
p
denote resp.
|x\rangle
,
|y\rangle
.
The verified program of
O
is as follows.

\begin{array}[]{clr}&\{|\varphi\rangle_{q}\langle\varphi|\}&\\
&\mathbf{Loc}\ p;\{|\varphi\rangle_{q}\langle\varphi|\otimes|0\rangle_{p}\langle 0|\}&\mbox{(R' Loc)}\\
&p\;{\ast}{=}\;HX;\{|\varphi\rangle_{q}\langle\varphi|\otimes|-\rangle_{p}\langle-|\}&\mbox{(A Unit)}\\
&(q,p)\;{\ast}{=}\;O;\{|\psi\rangle_{q}\langle\psi|\otimes|-\rangle_{p}\langle-|\}&\mbox{(A Unit)}\\
&p\;{\ast}{=}\;XH;\{|\psi\rangle_{q}\langle\psi|\otimes|0\rangle_{p}\langle 0|\}&\mbox{(A Unit)}\\
&\{|\psi\rangle_{q}\langle\psi|\otimes I_{p}\}&\mbox{(R Order)}\\
&\mathbf{Rel}\ p\ \{|\psi\rangle_{q}\langle\psi|\}&\mbox{(R' Loc)}\end{array}

6.2.
Quantum pointer passing

Due to the no-cloning theorem,
it’s impossible to realize all quantum value copying implicitly by a universal copying machine,
implemented as a unitary operator, as required by the principle of quantum mechanics.
The problem of parameter passing is well-understood
in the context of functional quantum programming languages,
and type systems for such quantum languages
usually rely on linear types and pointer-passing.
In other words, instead of passing values,
function calls pass wire identifiers, or register names.

Definition of the syntax.

We now extend
\mathit{RqPL}
with parameterized procedures.
The parameters consists of names of registers used in the global
environment that can be referred to inside the procedure.
A quantum program
P
with parameterized procedures now have the form of Tab.
9

(The extended programming language is coined
\mathit{eRqPL}
).
Note that the two lists of quantum variables
\bar{y}
and
\bar{p}
,
called, respectively, formal and actual parameters,
are required to have equal length and equal componentwise type.

Definition of the semantics.

The intended meaning of invoking a parameterized recursive procedure
is first expanding the body of the procedure with actual parameters in place of formal parameters,
which makes the procedure now able to act on already existing registers, and then executing this expanded body.
To define the formal semantics of parameterized procedures,
we can adapt the counterpart of non-parameterized procedures by adding syntactic substitution
(cf. Tabs.
9
and
9
),
where the syntactic approximation of the bodies of parameterized procedures can be defined
by parameterizing Def.
3.4
(cf. Def.
A.1
).

Proof rules for the correctness.

To parameterized recursive procedures
\mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})
with body
S_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq n
, the proof rule (Rp pRec) together with (R Adap)
can be used to prove their partial correctness;
the proof rule (Rt pRec) together with (R Adap)
can be used to prove their total correctness (cf. Tab.
9
).
Note that those non-parameterized proof rules for recursion,
e.g. (Rp gRec) defined in Tab.
5
,
can be thought of as a special case of their parameterized counterpart
by restricting the formal parameters
\bar{y}
to
\emptyset
.

Example 6.0.

Let the parameterized procedure
\mathit{proc}(\bar{p})
be defined by

\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{p})\colon\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{p};\ \mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{p}

The operational semantics of
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q})
is developed step by step as

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q}),\rho\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};\ \mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q},\rho\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}&\langle E,\rho\rangle\end{array}

The denotational semantics of
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q})
is defined by

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket(\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{p};\ \mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{p})^{(n)}[\bar{q}/\bar{p}]\rrbracket

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};\ \mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{tr}_{\bar{r}}\circ\llbracket\mathbf{skip}[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\rrbracket\circ(|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\diamond\langle 0|_{\bar{r}})=I_{\bar{q}}

To prove the Hoare’s triple

\{P_{\bar{q}}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q})\,\{P_{\bar{q}}\}\quad\big(\mbox{resp. }\langle P_{\bar{q}}\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{q})\,\langle P_{\bar{q}}\rangle\big)

by (R Adap), it suffices to prove

\{P_{\bar{p}}\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{p})\,\{P_{\bar{p}}\}

By (Rp pRec)
(
resp. (Rt pRec)
)
, it suffices to prove

\{P_{\bar{p}}\}\,\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{p};\ \mathbf{skip};\ \mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{p}\,\{P_{\bar{p}}\}

following by (R Loc), together with (A Skip), (A Init) and (R Comp).

6.3.
Extension of previous results

Various results developed in previous sections,
e.g. Thms.
3.7
,
4.15
,
5.4
,
5.6

and the soundness and completeness results (for both partial and total correctness),
can be extended to covering the case of auxiliary facilities discussed in this section
(cf. Apps.
A
,
B
and
D
).

7.
Case studies

7.1.
Grover’s fixed-point search

\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{qSearch}\colon S

S

\triangleq

\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q_{1}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}

S_{0}

\triangleq

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;V

S_{1}

\triangleq

q_{1}\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{-1};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch};

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;R_{t};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag};

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;R_{s};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch};

q_{1}\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{+1}

(a)

\mathit{qSearch}

\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag}\colon S^{\prime}

S^{\prime}

\triangleq

\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q_{1}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}^{\prime}\ \mathbf{fi}

S_{0}^{\prime}

\triangleq

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;V^{\dagger}

S_{1}^{\prime}

\triangleq

q_{1}\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{-1};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag};

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;R_{s}^{{\dagger}};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch};

q_{2}\;{\ast}{=}\;R_{t}^{{\dagger}};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag};

q_{1}\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{+1}

(b)

\mathit{qSearch\_dag}

Table 10
.

\mathit{qSearch}
and
\mathit{qSearch\_dag}
implement the search engine
V_{n}
and its adjoint
V_{n}^{{\dagger}}
.

Grover’s search is a quantum algorithm of finding a target item in an unsorted database,
which has a square-root speedup over the corresponding classical algorithm.
The original idea is to design an iterative transformation
in a way that each iteration results in a small rotation of the moving state
in a two-dimensional plane spanned by the (orthogonal) target and nontarget vectors.
The moving state rotates in the plane from a starting state to the target state.
If we choose the right number of iterative steps,
the moving state will stop close to the target state, otherwise it will drift away.
Fixed-point Grover’s search supplements the original search algorithm by permitting
the moving state converges monotonically to the target state as the number of iteration goes from zero to infinity.
This feature leads to robust search algorithms and also to new schemes for quantum control and error correction
(
Grover 2005
)
.

Programming the algorithm.

Let
|s\rangle
and
|t\rangle
be the respective starting and target states in a Hilbert space,
where
|s\rangle
is possibly superposed,
and
|t\rangle
is a (not necessarily uniform) superposition of all possible solutions.
The core of the algorithm is to design a search engine
— a series of unitary operators
\{V_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
inductively defined by

\begin{array}[]{cc}V_{0}\triangleq V,&V_{n+1}\triangleq V_{n}R_{s}V_{n}^{{\dagger}}R_{t}V_{n}\end{array}

where the
\frac{\pi}{3}
-phase shifts (i.e., unitary operators)
R_{s}
and
R_{t}
for
|s\rangle
and
|t\rangle
are defined as

\begin{array}[]{cc}R_{s}\triangleq I-\big(1-\exp(i\frac{\pi}{3})\big)|s\rangle\langle s|,&R_{t}\triangleq I-\big(1-\exp(i\frac{\pi}{3})\big)|t\rangle\langle t|\end{array}

such that the resulting state
V_{n}|s\rangle
after applying
V_{n}
to
|s\rangle

converges monotonically to
|t\rangle
as
n
approaches infinity, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}{V_{n}|s\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|t\rangle

Then we are able to fetch information of the solution
|t\rangle
by a measurement on
V_{n}|s\rangle
.
Note that
|t\rangle
can be thought of as the least fixed point of a function induced by
\big\{V_{n}|s\rangle\big\}_{n\geq 0}
.
This is the reason why this version of Grover’s search is called fixed-point Grover’s search.
For the sake of simplicity,
|s\rangle
,
|t\rangle
and
V
are treated as black boxes.

To program the search engine,
let us use quantum variable
q_{1}
to denote the moving state from
|s\rangle
to
|t\rangle
.
To model the counter of the search engine (used to denote the subscript
n
of
V_{n}
),
we shall use quantum variable
q_{2}
over a
2^{m}
-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{c}

with orthonormal basis states
\big\{|n\rangle\colon 0\leq n<2^{m}\big\}
,
which can be used to encode an upper-bounded set of natural numbers.
Here
m
should be large enough
so that the basis states of
\mathcal{H}_{c}
suffice to encode all needed counter values.
We define
(+i)
-operator
U_{+i}
of
\mathcal{H}_{c}
by

\begin{array}[]{c}U_{+i}\colon|x\rangle\rightarrow|(x+i)\mod 2^{m}\rangle,\end{array}

to model the classical modular
(+i)
-operator,
and similarly for
(-i)
-operator
U_{-i}
.
Whether the value of the counter is zero can be identified by the outcome of the measurement

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{M_{0}\triangleq|0\rangle\langle 0|,\ M_{1}\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{2^{m}-1}|i\rangle\langle i|\bigg\}

Recursive quantum procedure
\mathit{qSearch}
for the search engine is designed in Tab.
10
.

Partial correctness.

We claim that, on input
|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\otimes|s\rangle_{q_{2}}
,
quantum activation statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}
executes with output
|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\otimes V_{n}|s\rangle_{q_{2}}
(if terminates).
Formally speaking, the claim can be expressed as a partial-correctness formula:

(7)

\big\{|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|\otimes|s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|\big\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\big\{|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|\otimes V_{n}|s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|V_{n}^{\dagger}\big\}

Let
A
be a quantum predicate variable on
\mathcal{H}_{c}
, and
B
a quantum predicate variable on
\mathcal{H}_{s}
.
The
(i,j)
-component
\langle i|A|j\rangle
of
A
is abbreviated as
A_{i,j}
,
so
A=\sum_{i,j}A_{i,j}|i\rangle\langle j|
.
To prove Hoare’s triple (
7
),
by (R Subst), together with the simultaneous substitution

\big[|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|/A,\ |s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|/B\big]

it suffices to prove

\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\}

(
Intuitively, the precondition (resp. postcondition) of the last Hoare’s triple says that
the control flow arrives at each recursion depth
0\leq i\leq 2^{m}-1
(denoted by variable
q_{1}
) of procedure
\mathit{qSearch}
with probability
A_{i,i}
, and at depth
i
, the state of variable
q_{2}
should satisfy
the predicate
B
(resp.
V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}
).
)
Defining
\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}\big\}_{i=0,1}
by

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag}\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}^{\dagger}BV_{i}\bigg\}

by (Rp gRec), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}\big\}_{i=0,1}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\}\,S\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}\big\}_{i=0,1}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\}\,S^{\prime}\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}^{\dagger}BV_{i}\bigg\}

The routine verification work is left to App.
E.3
.

Total correctness.

We claim that quantum activation statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}
, on input
|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\otimes|s\rangle_{q_{2}}
,
always terminates with output
|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\otimes V_{n}|s\rangle_{q_{2}}
.
In a formal way, the claim can be expressed as a total-correctness formula:

(8)

\big\langle|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|\otimes|s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|\big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\big\langle|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|\otimes V_{n}|s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|V_{n}^{\dagger}\big\rangle

Let quantum predicate variables
A
and
B
be as defined above.
To prove Hoare’s triple (
8
), by (R Subst),
together with the simultaneous substitution

\big[|n\rangle_{q_{1}}\langle n|/A,\ |s\rangle_{q_{2}}\langle s|/B\big]

it suffices to prove

\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B\bigg\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\rangle

Defining a sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\big\{P_{j}[A,B]\big\}_{j\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
by

\displaystyle P_{j}[A,B]

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{i=0}^{j}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B&\hbox{if $0\leq j<2^{m}$}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes B&\hbox{if $j\geq 2^{m}$}\end{array}\right.

and a set of premises
\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}^{j}\big\}_{i=1,2}^{j\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{1}^{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\langle P_{j}[A,B]\bigg\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch}\,\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{2}^{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\langle P_{j}[A,B]\bigg\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{qSearch\_dag}\,\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}^{\dagger}BV_{i}\bigg\rangle

by (Rt gRec), it suffices to show, for all
j\geq 0
, that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}^{j}\big\}_{i=1,2}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}

\displaystyle\bigg\langle P_{j+1}[A,B]\bigg\rangle\,S\,\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}BV_{i}^{\dagger}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{i}^{j}\big\}_{i=1,2}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBS}}}

\displaystyle\bigg\langle P_{j+1}[A,B]\bigg\rangle\,S^{\prime}\,\bigg\langle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}A_{i,i}|i\rangle\langle i|\otimes V_{i}^{\dagger}BV_{i}\bigg\rangle

The routine verification work is left to App.
E.4
.

7.2.
Recursive quantum Fourier sampling

\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{RQFS}(q,Y)\colon\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}

S_{0}

\triangleq

q\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{+1};

\mathbf{Loc}\ \mathbb{X}[q],Y^{\prime};

\big(\mathbb{X}[q],Y^{\prime}\big)\;{\ast}{=}\;H^{\otimes n}\otimes HX;

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}(q,Y^{\prime});

\mathbb{X}[q]\;{\ast}{=}\;H^{\otimes n};

\big(\mathbb{X}[q],Y\big)\;{\ast}{=}\;\mathcal{G};

\mathbb{X}[q]\;{\ast}{=}\;H^{\otimes n};

\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}(q,Y^{\prime});

\big(\mathbb{X}[q],Y^{\prime}\big)\;{\ast}{=}\;H^{\otimes n}\otimes XH;

\mathbf{Rel}\ \mathbb{X}[q],Y^{\prime};

q\;{\ast}{=}\;U_{-1}

S_{1}

\triangleq

\big(\mathbb{X}[1],\ldots,\mathbb{X}[l],Y\big)\;{\ast}{=}\;\mathcal{O}

S_{2}

\triangleq

\mathbf{bot}

Table 11
.
Recursive quantum procedure
\mathit{RQFS}
.

Problem description.

Let us first briefly recall recursive quantum Fourier sampling
(
McKague 2012
)
.
We begin by defining a type of tree.
Let
n,l
be positive integers and consider a symmetric tree where each node,
except the leaves, has
2^{n}
children, and the depth is
l
.
Let the root be labelled by
(\emptyset)
.
The root’s children are labelled
(x_{1})
with
x_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
Each child of
(x_{1})
is, in turn, labelled
(x_{1},x_{2})
with
x_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
We continue until we have reached the leaves, which are labelled by
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
.

Next we add the Fourier component to the tree.
We begin by fixing a computable function
g\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
.
With each node of the tree
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})

we associate a “secret” string
s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
s.t.

\displaystyle g(s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})}\cdot x_{k}\mod 2

(Here we take
s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})}
to mean
s_{(\emptyset)}
if
k=1
.)
In this way, each node’s secret encodes one bit of information about its parent’s secret.
Suppose we are given an oracle
o\colon(\{0,1\}^{n})^{l}\rightarrow\{0,1\}

for the leaves of the tree s.t.

\displaystyle o(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle g\big(s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})}\big)

Our goal is to find
g(s_{(\emptyset)})
.

Quantum solution.

Define the descendant space
\mathcal{H}_{d}
to be the
2^{n}
-dimensional Hilbert space with orthonormal basis states
—
\big\{|i\rangle\colon 0\leq i<2^{n}\big\}
— to index each of
2^{n}
children for any parental node.
The counting space
\mathcal{H}_{c}
,
(+i)
-operator
U_{+i}
and
(-i)
-operator
U_{-i}
of
\mathcal{H}_{c}

are defined as previous (Here, to index the depth of the tree, we require that
l<2^{m}
).

Let
p,q
be quantum (individual) variables over
\mathcal{H}_{c}
,

Y,Y^{\prime},Z
quantum variables over
\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
and
\mathbb{X}
a quantum array-like variable over
\mathcal{H}_{d}

with one argument, say
q
, indexing each component of the array,
s.t. each component
\mathbb{X}[q]
acts like a quantum variable over
\mathcal{H}_{d}
.
We shall treat
\mathbb{X}[|i\rangle]
as
\mathbb{X}[i]
for simplicity.
Let quantum oracle
\mathcal{G}

(
on
\mathbb{X}[q]
,
\mathit{Y}
)
calculate
g
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\ |s\rangle|y\rangle

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle|s\rangle|y\oplus g(s)\rangle

Let quantum oracle
\mathcal{O}

(
on
\mathbb{X}[1],\ldots,\mathbb{X}[l]
,
\mathit{Y}
)
model
o
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\ |x_{1}\rangle\ldots|x_{l}\rangle|y\rangle

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle|x_{1}\rangle\ldots|x_{l}\rangle|y\oplus g(s_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})})\rangle

The procedure for Recursive quantum Fourier sampling is designed in Tab.
11
,
where the measurement
M
(on
q
) is defined by

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigg\{M_{0}\triangleq\sum_{0\leq i<l}|i\rangle\langle i|,\ M_{1}\triangleq|l\rangle\langle l|,\ M_{2}\triangleq\sum_{l<i<2^{m}}|i\rangle\langle i|\bigg\}

Notations and Definitions.

Let
K
be a quantum predicate variable over
\mathcal{H}_{c}
.
Let
\big\{|i\rangle\big\}_{i}
be the computational basis of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
.
For notational convenience, the
(i,i)
-component
\langle i|K|i\rangle
of
K
is abbreviated as
K_{i}
.
(To see the intuitive meaning of
K_{i}
, we remark that
K=\sum_{i,j}\langle i|K|j\rangle|i\rangle\langle j|
.)
Define
C(i)
and
D(i)
by

\displaystyle C(i)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=0}^{i}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{2^{n}-1}|k\rangle_{x_{j}}\langle k|\otimes\alpha_{j}\Big)

\displaystyle D(i)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=0}^{i}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{2^{n}-1}|k\rangle_{x_{j}}\langle k|\otimes\beta_{j}\Big)

where
\alpha_{j}
and
\beta_{j}
are defined by

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|0\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle 0|,&\hbox{if $j=0$}\\
|-\rangle_{y_{j}}\langle-|,&\hbox{if $1\leq j\leq l$}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\beta_{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|g(s_{(\emptyset)})\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle g(s_{(\emptyset)})|,&\hbox{if $j=0$}\\
|-\rangle_{y_{j}}\langle-|,&\hbox{if $1\leq j\leq l$}\end{array}\right.

Define the
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
P(K)
and
Q(K)
by

\displaystyle P(K)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{l}K_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|\otimes C(i)

\displaystyle Q(K)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{l}K_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|\otimes D(i)

Intuitively,
P(K)

(
resp.
Q(K)
)
says that the control flow arrives
at each recursion depth
0\leq i\leq l
(denoted by variable
q
) of algorithm
\mathit{RQFS}
with probability
K_{i}

(where
i
corresponds to each level of the tree, in particular,
i=0
points to the root and
i=l
to the leaves),
and at depth
i
, variable
\mathbb{Y}[0]
lies in the state
|0\rangle

(
resp.
|g(s_{(\emptyset)})\rangle
)
,

\mathbb{Y}[j]
with
1\leq j\leq i
in
|-\rangle
,
and
\mathbb{X}[j]
with
0\leq j\leq i
can lie in any state (by the predicate
I_{x_{j}}
).

Partial correctness.

We claim the partial correctness of
\mathit{RQFS}
by proving Hoare’s triple

\big\{|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\otimes|0\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle 0|\big\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}\big(q,\mathbb{Y}[q]\big)\,\big\{|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\otimes|g(s_{(\emptyset)})\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle g(s_{(\emptyset)})|\big\}

By (R Subst), together with the substitution
\big[|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|/K\big]
, it suffices to show

(\mathit{Prem}\triangleq\>)\{P(K)\}\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}\big(q,\mathbb{Y}[q]\big)\,\{Q(K)\}

By (Rp pRec), it suffices to show

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\mathit{Prem}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{\mathit{qBE}}}

\displaystyle\{P(K)\}\,\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}\,\{Q(K)\}

The routine verification work is left to App.
F.3
.

Total correctness.

We claim the total correctness of
\mathit{RQFS}
by proving Hoare’s triple

\big\langle|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\otimes|0\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle 0|\big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}\big(q,\mathbb{Y}[q]\big)\,\big\langle|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\otimes|g(s_{(\emptyset)})\rangle_{y_{0}}\langle g(s_{(\emptyset)})|\big\rangle

By (R Subst), together with the substitution
\big[|0\rangle_{q}\langle 0|/K\big]
, it suffices to show

\langle P(K)\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}\big(q,\mathbb{Y}[q]\big)\,\langle Q(K)\rangle

Defining a sequence of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s
\big\{P_{h}(K)\big\}_{h\geq 0}^{\sqsubseteq}
by

\displaystyle P_{h}(K)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{i=l+1-h}^{l}K_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|\otimes C(i)&\hbox{$0\leq h<l$}\\
P(K),&\hbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

and a set of premises
\big\{\mathit{Prem}_{h}\big\}_{h\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\mathit{Prem}_{h}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\big\langle P_{h}(K)\big\rangle\,\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{RQFS}\big(q,\mathbb{Y}[q]\big)\,\big\langle Q(K)\big\rangle

by (Rt pRec), it suffices to show, for all
h\geq 0
, that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathit{\sqsubseteq}},\mathit{Prem}_{h}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\mathit{qBE}}

\displaystyle\big\langle P_{h+1}(K)\big\rangle\,\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[q]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}\,\big\langle Q(K)\big\rangle

The routine verification work is left to App.
F.4
.

8.
Related work

In this section, we will compare our quantum Hoare logic (QHL)
with classical deterministic Hoare logic (DHL), classical probabilistic Hoare logic (PHL), and other QHLs.
We shall discuss global and local reasoning in the setting of quantum computation.
Comparison with other related work, e.g. on termination and loop invariants etc., is also presented.

8.1.
Comparison with DHL

Verification techniques for classical (deterministic) recursive programs
have been systematically developed since Hoare’s pioneering work
(
Hoare 1971
)

(see, e.g., Chaps. 4, 5 of
(
Apt
et al. 2009
)
and Chap. 6 of
(
Francez 1992
)
).
However, the semantics of quantum programs and quantum assertions are complex-matrix-based,
neither relations nor formulas on a discrete space as in the classical case.
This semantical difference entails that
there is no uniform mechanism for encoding any finite computational sequence of quantum programs
(For classical coding functions, e.g. Gödel’s
\beta
-function and pairing functions,
the reader is referred to
(
Boolos
et al. 2002
)
),
so quantum logics have no closed (finite) form as in classical logics.
To address the challenge, we have to accept an infinite representation of program semantics and assertions,
e.g. the upper and lower limits,
and try to find a closed-form (e.g. fixed-point) characterization for them (cf. Rem.
3.3
).

8.2.
Comparison with PHL

A theory of probabilistic recursion and recursive probabilistic programming
has recently been developed in a series of papers
(
Lago
et al. 2014
;
Breuvart
et al. 2017
)
.
In the last few years, significant progress has been made in verifying recursive probabilistic programs,
including weakest pre-expectation calculus and proof rules
(
Olmedo
et al. 2016
;
Kaminski et al. 2018
)

as well as termination problem
(
Kobayashi
et al. 2020
)
.
We remark that the assertion languages of
(
Olmedo
et al. 2016
;
Kaminski et al. 2018
;
Barthe et al. 2018
)
have no parameters.
The lack of parameters would restrict the scope of application of their logics to the case of tail recursion
(cf. Exms
5.1
,
5.2
and Rem.
5.7
).
The introduction of parameters for random variables,
which can be thought of as a probabilistic degenerate of quantum predicate variables proposed in this paper,
could help to repair the technical deficiency for the sake of completeness.
Note that the termination problem of probabilistic higher-order programs
(
Kobayashi
et al. 2020
)

is reduced to the probabilistic reachability problem of a higher-order extension of recursive Markov chains
(not in an axiomatic way).

8.3.
Comparison with other QHLs

Other quantum Hoare-like logics,
e.g.
(
Chadha
et al. 2006
;
Feng
et al. 2007
;
Kakutani 2009
)
,
have been developed with the Turing-Floyd-Hoare principle.
For the landscape of some of these quantum logics, the reader is referred to the survey paper
(
Rand 2019
)
.
The assertion languages of these QHLs surveyed in
(
Rand 2019
)
are compositional
and contain parameters, yet are not purely quantum (matrix-based).
It’s worthy to note that a kind of (existentially quantified) ghost variables,
which can be entangled with program variables,
are introduced in the assertion language of
(
Unruh 2019b
)
.
In contrast, the quantum predicate variables defined in the current paper
are free higher-order variables (if program variables are seen as first-order variables),
and can be used to describe properties of entangled program variables
(yet quantum predicate variables never entangle with program variables).
The ghost variables can be used to simulate local variables,
but the interaction between ghost and program variables is uncontrolled due to existential quantification,
thus Unruh’s QHL fails to reason about a particular quantum operation as in Exm.
6.1
.
The paper
(
Hung
et al. 2019
)
develops a formal semantics for erroneous quantum
\mathbf{while}
-programs,
as well as a logic for reasoning about their robustness (i.e. error bounds of outputs).
We remark that assertions of this QHL are
\mathrm{QPRD}
s,
and that inference rules for a while loop are not designed in a purely syntactical way.
An applied QHL
(
Zhou
et al. 2019
)
(also supporting reasoning about robustness of quantum programs)
is defined by restricting assertions of QHL
(
Ying 2011
)
(i.e.
\mathrm{QPRD}
s)
to subspaces of a Hilbert space (i.e. projection operators).
Thus the applied QHL fails to do reasoning with probabilities
\delta\in(0,1)

(cf. Rem.
5.5
for a detailed comparison).

8.4.
Comparison with local reasoning

Hoare logic is a general framework for global reasoning about programs.
As an alternative technical line,
local reasoning is proposed by using the Frame Rule under the assumption that
the underlying program states be separated
(in, e.g., separation logic
(
Reynolds 2002
;
Batz et al. 2019
)

and quantum relational Hoare logic
(
Unruh 2019a
;
Barthe
et al. 2020
)
).
In principle, the scope of applicability of global reasoning is larger than that of local reasoning.
In practice, local reasoning could bring some advantages, e.g. simpler semantics and shorter assertions,
leading to a good trade-off between application scope and program scale.
However, in the quantum field, global reasoning is indispensable,
since sometimes a global quantum state should be treated as an inseparable entity (e.g. an entangled state),
in which case a global assertion (e.g. an inseparable Hermitian matrix) should be used instead of concatenating local assertions by using the Frame Rule.

8.5.
Comparison with other related work

The (almost-sure) termination problem of quantum programs
(
Li and Ying 2018
)

is reduced to the realisability and synthesis problem of linear ranking super-martingales,
which can be solved by resorting to an SDP (Semi-Definite Programming) solver
(This approach comes from the constraint-based solution to the termination problem of probabilistic programs
(
Chakarov and
Sankaranarayanan 2013
;
Chatterjee et al. 2016
;
McIver
et al. 2017
;
Kaminski
et al. 2019
)
).
In contrast, this paper proposes an axiomatic approach to
the probabilistic (including almost-sure) termination problem of quantum programs.
Characterizations and generation of loop invariants of quantum programs
(
Ying
et al. 2017
)

have been developed in a framework of super-operator-valued transition system based on SDP.
By contrast, this paper discusses the existence issue of
a uniform fixed-point characterisation for (general) recursive invariants,
and explains why the synthesis of recursive invariants can not be automated completely.
A theorem prover
(
Liu et al. 2019
)
for verifying partial correctness of quantum
\mathbf{while}
-programs
using QHL
(
Ying 2011
)
has been developed within the framework of Isabelle/HOL
(A detailed partial-correctness proof of Grover’s original search algorithm is implemented thereof).
Quantum relational Hoare logic
(
Unruh 2019a
;
Barthe
et al. 2020
)

allows to reason about how the outputs of two quantum programs relate given a relation between their inputs.
Finally, quantum Hoare type theory
(
Singhal 2020
)
is inspired by classical Hoare type theory
and extends Quantum IO Monad by indexing it with pre- and post-conditions that serve as program specifications,
which has the potential to be a unified system for programming, specifying, and reasoning about quantum programs.

9.
Conclusion and future work

This paper has systematically investigated the problem of how to verify
parameterized recursive quantum programs with ancilla data and probabilistic control.
We have defined a new quantum assertion logic, a parameterized extension of quantum predicates,
so that, by using formulas of this assertion logic as pre- and post-conditions,
Hoare’s approach for both partial and total correctness can be extended to the case of general recursive procedures
(i.e. soundness and completeness of our quantum Hoare logic).
The assertion logic makes it realizable to reduce
reasoning about quantum programs with both approximate and exact probabilities
to a total-correctness proof.
In particular, two counterexamples for illustrating
incompleteness of quantum predicates in verifying recursive procedures,
and, one counterexample for showing the failure of reasoning with exact probabilities based on partial correctness,
have also been constructed.
The usefulness of the quantum Hoare logic has been illustrated by three main examples:
recursive quantum Markov chain (with probabilistic control),
fixed-point Grover’s search,
and recursive quantum Fourier sampling.

For the future work, we find that satisfiability of Löwner order (resp. equality) on the quantum assertion logic
has to be reduced to positivity (resp. equality) of super operators on separable states (cf. Rem.
4.3
).
Note that complete positivity of a super operator can be reduced to positivity of a linear operator
(called Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism or Channel-state duality;
for more information on this topic, cf., e.g.,
(
Wolf 2012
)
).
However, a useful characterization of positivity (resp. equality) of super operators is still out of reach
(For more information on this area of research, cf., e.g.,
(
Johnston [n.d.]a
;
Johnston [n.d.]b
)
).
We could also consider introducing quantifiers into the assertion logic.
It would be interesting to compare this logic with other quantum logics,
e.g. linear logic
(
Girard 1987
)
(a logic for a linear use of quantum resources),
within the framework of orthomodular lattices or category theory.
On the other hand, quantum programs with quantum control have been studied in a series of previous work

(
see, e.g.,
(
Altenkirch and
Grattage 2005
)
, Chaps. 6 and 7 of
(
Ying 2016
)
,

(
Badescu and
Panangaden 2015
;
Sabry
et al. 2018
)
)
,
and the notions of quantum recursion with quantum control were already introduced there.
However, we are still at a very beginning stage of understanding quantum recursions with quantum controls,
and we feel that a program logic for reasoning about them requires some ideas very different from those used in this paper.

Appendix A
Parameterized recursive quantum programs

This section is devoted to investigating the formal semantics of parameterized recursive quantum programs
\mathit{eRqPL}
.
Formally,
\mathit{eRqPL}
can be defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}P&\triangleq&D::S&\mbox{Quantum program}\\
D&\triangleq&\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{1}\rangle(\bar{y}_{1})\colon S_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Proc}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{n}\rangle(\bar{y}_{n})\colon S_{n}&\mbox{Procedure declaration}\\
S&\triangleq&\mathbf{bot}&\mbox{Bottom}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{skip}&\mbox{No operation}\\
&\mid&q:=|0\rangle&\mbox{Initialization}\\
&\mid&\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U&\mbox{Unitary operation}\\
&\mid&S_{1};S_{2}&\mbox{Sequential composition}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi}&\mbox{Probabilistic branching}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}&\mbox{Variable localization}\\
&\mid&\mathbf{call}\ \langle\mathit{proc}_{i}\rangle(\bar{p}_{i}),\quad 1\leq i\leq n&\mbox{Procedure call}\end{array}

A.1.
Nondeterministic operational semantics

(Bot)

\langle\mathbf{bot},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,0\rangle

(Skip)

\langle\mathbf{skip},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,\rho\rangle

(Init)

\dfrac{\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle i|=I_{q}}{\langle q:=|0\rangle,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,\sum_{i}|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|\rho|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\rangle}

(Unit)

\dfrac{UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=I_{\bar{q}}}{\langle\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,U\rho U^{\dagger}\rangle}

(Comp
1
)

\dfrac{\langle S_{1},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{1}^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\rangle\mbox{ and }S_{1}^{\prime}\neq E}{\langle S_{1};S_{2},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{1}^{\prime};S_{2},\rho^{\prime}\rangle}

(Comp
2
)

\dfrac{\langle S_{1},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}{\langle S_{1};S_{2},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\langle S_{2},\rho^{\prime}\rangle}

(Case)

\dfrac{M=\{M_{m}\}_{m}\mbox{ and }\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}M_{m}=I_{\bar{q}}}{\langle\mathbf{if}\ \Box m\cdot M[\bar{q}]=m\rightarrow S_{m}\ \mathbf{fi},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{m}\langle S_{m},M_{m}\rho M_{m}^{\dagger}\rangle}

(Loc)

\dfrac{\bar{r}\not\in\mathit{Var}(\rho),\ |\bar{r}|=|\bar{q}|,\ \it type(r_{i})=\it type(q_{i})\ \forall\ i.}{\langle\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}];\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle}

(Rel)

\dfrac{tr_{\bar{r}}\triangleq\sum_{i}\langle i|\diamond|i\rangle\mbox{ with }\sum_{i}|i\rangle\langle i|=I_{\bar{r}}}{\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle E,tr_{\bar{r}}(\rho)\rangle}

(Proc)

\dfrac{\mathbf{Proc}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{y})\colon S\in D}{\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}(\bar{p}),\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\langle S[\bar{p}/\bar{y}],\rho\rangle}

(Except)

\dfrac{\mbox{Other cases of $S$ and $l$}}{\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{l}\bot}

Table 12
.
Labeled transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}
with
l\triangleq\epsilon\mid m
.

The transition relation
\xrightarrow{l}
for
\mathit{eRqPL}
is defined in Tab.
12
.
The multi-step labeled transition relation
\xrightarrow{\alpha}

with
\alpha\triangleq l\mid\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
can be defined on
\xrightarrow{l}
as before.

A.2.
\mathrm{QOP}
-directed denotational semantics

(Bot)

\llbracket\mathbf{bot}\rrbracket=0\diamond 0

(Skip)

\llbracket\mathbf{skip}\rrbracket=I\diamond I

(Init)

\llbracket q:=|0\rangle\rrbracket=\sum_{i}|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|\diamond|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|

(Unit)

\llbracket\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U\rrbracket=U\diamond U^{\dagger}

(Comp)

\llbracket S_{1};S_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket

(Case)

\llbracket\mathbf{if}\rrbracket=\sum_{m}\llbracket S_{m}\rrbracket\circ(M_{m}\diamond M_{m}^{\dagger})

(Loc)

\llbracket\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\rrbracket=\mathit{tr}_{\bar{r}}\circ\llbracket S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\rrbracket\circ(|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\diamond\langle 0|_{\bar{r}})

(Proc)

\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\rrbracket=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}]\rrbracket

Table 13
.
Denotational semantics of
\mathit{eRqPL}
.

The denotational semantics of a quantum program, denoted
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
, is defined as a
\mathrm{QOP}
.
The semantics of each term is given in a compositional way, except for the case of parameterized call statements.
To handle this case, we need to define the syntactic approximation (i.e., unrolling) of the bodies
of mutually recursive procedures with parameter passing.

Definition A.0 (Parameterized approximation).

Let
\mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})\in D
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be parameterized recursive quantum procedure with body
S_{i}
.
Let
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}(\bar{p}_{j_{k}})
,
1\leq j\leq n
, be any parameterized call statement inside
S_{i}

(
Here
k
means
S_{i}
has (possibly different) activations of
\mathit{proc}_{j}
each parameterized
\bar{p}_{j_{k}}
)
.
Then the
k
th syntactic approximation
S_{i}^{(k)}
of
S_{i}
is defined as:

\displaystyle S_{i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathbf{bot}

\displaystyle S_{i}^{(k+1)}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle S_{i}\big[\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}[\bar{p}_{j_{k}}/\bar{y}_{j}]\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}(\bar{p}_{j_{k}}),\ldots\big]

where
S_{i}\big[\ldots,\big(\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}[\bar{p}_{j_{k}}/\bar{y}_{j}]\big)\big/\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}(\bar{p}_{j_{k}}),\ldots\big]

stands for simultaneous substitution of the statement
\mathbf{skip};S_{j}^{(k)}[\bar{p}_{j_{k}}/\bar{y}_{j}]

for every
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}(\bar{p}_{j_{k}})
in
S_{i}

(
Here
\mathbf{skip}
is used to simulate the first-step transition
\xrightarrow{\epsilon}

for the statement
\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{j}(\bar{p}_{j_{k}})
, cf. the (Skip, Proc) rules of Tab.
12
)
.

The denotational semantics
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
for
\mathit{eRqPL}[\Omega]
is defined in Tab.
13
.
Note that the side conditions for many equations of denotational semantics are omitted,
because they follow the same ones as in the rules of operational semantics.
By extending Lem.
3.5
to the parameterized case,
well-definedness of the (Proc) rule in Tab.
13
follows.

A.3.
Connection between the two semantics

As a preliminary, we need to prove two structural properties of the operational semantics
for the syntactic approximation of a parameterized recursive procedure.

Lemma A.0
.

Let
S_{i}
be the body of procedure
\mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})
with
1\leq i\leq n
.

(1):

For any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and
\alpha
, we have that

\displaystyle\langle S_{i},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\exists k\geq 0.\ \langle S_{i}^{(k)},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle

(2):

Suppose, for some
k\geq 0
, that

\displaystyle\langle S_{i}^{(k)},\rho\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\alpha}

\displaystyle\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle

Then we have, for all
l\geq k
, that

\displaystyle\langle S_{i}^{(l)},\rho\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\alpha}

\displaystyle\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the length
|\alpha|
of
\alpha
,
and do a case analysis for the last step of the inductive definition of
S_{i}
.
∎

Remark A.1.

Lem.
A.2
says that every execution of
S_{i}
(labeled by
\alpha
)
can be simulated in a finite unrolling of
S_{i}
(denoted by
S_{i}^{(k)}
),
and also in any larger unrolling of
S_{i}
(denoted by
S_{i}^{(l)}
with
l\geq k
).
Note that, by the least number principle, there exists the least such
k
.

We are now positioned to relate the operational and denotational semantics.

Theorem A.3
.

For any quantum program
S\in\mathit{eRqPL}
, we have that

\displaystyle\llbracket S\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}

where the summation of
\rho^{\prime}
is taken for every possible
\alpha
s.t.
\langle S,\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof.

The proof can be done by induction on the depth of the formation tree of
S
.
We only consider the cases of variable localization and parameterized activation

(
The proof of other cases can be adapted from the corresponding proof in
(
Ying 2011
)
)
.

Case: (Loc).

(13)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[tr_{\bar{r}}\circ\llbracket S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\rrbracket\circ(|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\diamond\langle 0|_{\bar{r}})\big](\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tr_{\bar{r}}\big(\llbracket S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\rrbracket(\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tr_{\bar{r}}\Big(\sum_{\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}],\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}\Big)

(14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}],\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}tr_{\bar{r}}(\rho^{\prime})

(15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}],\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(\sum_{\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho^{\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\langle E,\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime\prime}\Big)

(16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}];\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(\sum_{\langle\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho^{\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\langle E,\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime\prime}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}];\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{r},\rho\otimes|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}\langle 0|\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q},\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\langle E,\rho^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime\prime}

where Eq. (
13
) follows by induction hypothesis applied to
S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]
,
(
14
) by linearity of partial trace function,
(
15
) by (Rel) rule of operational semantics,
and (
16
) by (Comp
2
) rule of operational semantics.

Case: (Proc).

(17)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}]\rrbracket\big)(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}]\rrbracket(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{\langle S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}],\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}\Big)

(18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle S_{i}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}],\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}

(19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\langle\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i}),\rho\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}\langle E,\rho^{\prime}\rangle}\rho^{\prime}

where Eq. (
17
) follows from a nonrecursive version of this theorem (proved similarly),
(
18
) by Lem.
A.2
,
and (
19
) by (Proc) rule of operational semantics.
∎

Appendix B
Quantum assertion language and expressiveness

B.1.
Formal semantics of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s

Definition B.0 (Semantics of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s).

Follow symbols and notations in Def.
4.1
.
Recall that
\mathbb{I}
is the standard interpretation
from constant and function symbols in the syntax of
\mathrm{PQPT}
s to their semantic counterparts,
and
v
is an assignment s.t.
v(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}})
.

The denotation of
P_{\bar{q}}
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
and assignment
v
,
denoted
P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}
, is defined as

\displaystyle P_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v})+\mathcal{F}_{\bar{q}}^{*}(I_{\bar{q}})

where
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}
,
the denotation of
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
and assignment
v
,
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}^{\mathbb{I},v}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}})&\hbox{if $\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}=\mathcal{X}_{\bar{q}}$}\\
I_{\bar{r}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}^{\mathbb{I},v}&\hbox{if $\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}=I_{\bar{r}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}$}\\
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}^{\mathbb{I},v}\otimes I_{\bar{s}}&\hbox{if $\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}=\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}\otimes I_{\bar{s}}$}\\
\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}^{\mathbb{I},v}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}^{\mathbb{I},v}&\hbox{if $\mathcal{B}_{\bar{q}}=\mathcal{B}_{\bar{r}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\bar{s}}$}\end{array}\right.

where a bit notational abuses between semantics (the left) and syntax (the right) are allowed, e.g., the left
I_{\bar{r}}
, or strictly
{I_{\bar{r}}}^{\mathbb{I}}
, is the denotation of the right
I_{\bar{r}}
under interpretation
\mathbb{I}
.

B.2.
Weakest (liberal) preconditions

\mathit{wp}.\big(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\big).P=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}].P

\mathit{wlp}.\big(\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\big).P=\bigsqcap_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathit{wlp}.S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}].P

\mathit{wp}.\mathbf{bot}.P=0

\mathit{wlp}.\mathbf{bot}.P=I

\mathit{xp}.\mathbf{skip}.P=P

\mathit{xp}.(q:=|0\rangle).P=\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|P|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|

\mathit{xp}.(\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U).P=U^{\dagger}PU

\mathit{xp}.(S_{1};S_{2}).P=\mathit{xp}.S_{1}.(\mathit{xp}.S_{2}.P)

\mathit{xp}.\mathbf{if}.P=\sum_{m}M_{m}^{\dagger}(\mathit{xp}.S_{m}.P)M_{m}

\mathit{xp}.(\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}).P=\langle 0|_{\bar{r}}\big(\mathit{xp}.S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}].(P\otimes I_{\bar{r}})\big)|0\rangle_{\bar{r}}

.

\mathit{xp}\in\{\mathit{wp},\mathit{wlp}\}
.

Theorem B.2

(Quantum expressiveness theorem)
.

Let quantum program
S\in\mathit{eRqPL}
, and
P
a
\mathrm{PQPT}
.
Define
\mathit{wp}.S.P
and
\mathit{wlp}.S.P
as in Tab.
14
.
(Note that by extending Lem.
4.16
to the parameterized case,
well-definedness of
\mathit{wp}
and
\mathit{wlp}
for parameterized activation follows.)
It is the case that

(a):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wp}.S.P=\llbracket S\rrbracket^{*}(P)
;

(b):

\models_{\mathbb{I}}\mathit{wlp}.S.P=I-\mathit{wp}.S.(I-P)
.

Proof.

Since
\llbracket S\rrbracket
can be seen as a
\mathrm{QOP}
,
by the inductive definition of
\llbracket S\rrbracket
(cf. Tab.
13
), together with Lem.
2.2
,
we are able to obtain an inductive definition of
\llbracket S\rrbracket^{*}
(cf. Tab.
15
).
Then Stat. (a) follows by induction on
S
, in which for the case of parameterized
\mathbf{call}
-statement,
it suffices to show, for all
n\geq 0
, that

(21)

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle\mathit{wp}.S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}].P=\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}]\rrbracket^{*}(P)

This is indeed the case because
S_{i}^{(n)}
is a non-recursive quantum program.

(
We can first prove a non-recursive version of Stat. (a) by induction on
S
.
)

Stat. (b) can be obtained similar to (a) by using the fact that
the G. L. B. operator
\bigsqcap
can be defined
as the logical dual of the L. U. B. operator
\bigsqcup
,
together with linearity of super operators.

\llbracket\mathbf{bot}\rrbracket^{*}=0\diamond 0

\llbracket\mathbf{skip}\rrbracket^{*}=I\diamond I

\llbracket q:=|0\rangle\rrbracket^{*}=\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{q}\langle 0|\diamond|0\rangle_{q}\langle i|

\llbracket\bar{q}\;{\ast}{=}\;U\rrbracket^{*}=U^{\dagger}\diamond U

\llbracket S_{1};S_{2}\rrbracket^{*}=\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket^{*}\circ\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket^{*}

\llbracket\mathbf{if}\rrbracket^{*}=\sum_{m}(M_{m}^{\dagger}\diamond M_{m})\circ\llbracket S_{m}\rrbracket^{*}

\llbracket\mathbf{Loc}\ \bar{q};S;\mathbf{Rel}\ \bar{q}\rrbracket^{*}=(\langle 0|_{\bar{r}}\diamond|0\rangle_{\bar{r}})\circ\llbracket S[\bar{r}/\bar{q}]\rrbracket^{*}\circ(I_{\bar{r}}\diamond I_{\bar{r}})

\llbracket\mathbf{call}\ \mathit{proc}_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})\rrbracket^{*}=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\llbracket S_{i}^{(n)}[\bar{a}_{i}/\bar{y}_{i}]\rrbracket^{*}

.
The inductive definition of
\llbracket S\rrbracket^{*}
